Var Energi · VAR-NO · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Var Energi

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,889 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F2054th of 55

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external picture is a supply-shock peak, not a demand upcycle. Prices are high because supply is short (Hormuz, storage, Russian phase-out), while underlying European gas demand keeps falling and oil demand has no cushion; the visibility of today's price is one to two winters. The consensus deck already assumes normalisation (about USD 80/boe realised in 2027 and USD 69/boe in 2028, derived), so external forces matter to the model in two ways: (1) they set the level of the deck the Consumer chooses, with a transmission of about USD 0.12-0.13 of EPS per USD 10/boe; and (2) they create a dated cluster of headwinds in 2027-2028 (LNG additions, end of the temporary tax uplift, cash-tax catch-up, krone strength) that the forward earnings decline already partly reflects. Inputs are a headwind that the company has not hedged. The tailwinds are strong now and largely priced; the headwinds are slower and more durable. For the model: use at least three explicit price decks (consensus-like normalisation, spot persisting through 2027, and a USD 60 Brent downside), and do not let the 2026 base annualise.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0242C-0528C-0733C-0940C-3582C-3930
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

D4228th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity in Var's output market is irrelevant (it is a price taker); intensity is rising in the markets Var buys from and in the market for replacement resources, which is where competition now costs it money.

  • Output side. Oil and gas prices are set globally; Norwegian shelf rivalry does not move them. The shelf has consolidated from 56 active companies (2013) to 25 (2024); conduct among the large players is cooperative (the Equinor, Aker BP and Var exploration collaboration to test 20-25 high-impact prospects over four to five years; asset swaps to align hubs).
  • Input side, rising. Equinor plans to raise Norwegian subsea tie-backs from two or three a year to six to eight; Aker BP is at peak spend (USD 6.8-7.2bn in 2026); global subsea equipment spend is expected to grow about 25% from 2025 to 2030 (Jefferies). Var competes with these better-capitalised, lower-cost operators for the same rigs, subsea systems, vessels and engineers.
  • Resource side, rising. Twenty-one companies applied in the 2026 APA licensing round, including Equinor, Aker BP, ConocoPhillips, Shell, TotalEnergies, DNO, Harbour and ORLEN; DNO (via Sval, now about 85 kboepd) and ORLEN (targeting 200,000 boe/d on the shelf) are becoming more active acquirers; Aker BP holds 183 recently awarded licences.
  • Direction for the model. Rising competition for inputs is part of why unit cost and capex per barrel are not falling; the effect is gradual, not a break.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F2849th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a clear and persistent headwind, compounded for Var by having no currency hedges; it is the main reason the cost-reduction trajectory stalled in 2026.

  • Rigs. Harsh-environment semi-submersible day rates USD 450,000-525,000 and possibly the low USD 500,000s with bonuses; only 22 semi-submersibles can work the shelf; Var has fixed Deepsea Bergen for 2028-2031 (USD 518m for three years per the Aker BP review), protecting schedule at elevated cost.
  • Subsea and installation. Three contractors control the subsea production system market; zero newbuild vessel capacity entering; SURF capacity capped through 2027-2028; Subsea 7 margins upgraded to about 24%; a proposed Saipem and Subsea 7 merger would concentrate supply further.
  • Labour and equipment. An engineering expert says 10-20% of projects are being resequenced because of skilled labour shortages; equipment lead times remain extended.
  • Currency. About 70% of opex and 60% of development capex is in NOK; Var held no FX hedges at end-2025 (Aker BP hedges 70-80% of NOK spend through 2027 at 10.5-11.0 NOK per USD). Nordea forecasts USD/NOK at 9.82 in mid-2027 falling to 9.19 by end-2027, i.e. a stronger krone. Management attributed about USD 0.5/boe of the year-to-date cost increase to FX.
  • Energy and carbon. Higher fuel and power prices were named in the cost-guidance raise; the carbon tax rises to 2030.
  • Mitigants. About 85% of near-term capex committed at FID; long-term alliances (SLB OneSubsea, Ocean Installer, Saipem, Halliburton, Aker Solutions, a five-year iEPCI collaboration with TechnipFMC); the Norwegian tax deduction absorbs most of a capex overrun (Aker BP: a USD 1.1bn pre-tax increase cost about USD 200m after tax).
  • Model implication. Keep unit production cost at about USD 11/boe with upside risk of about USD 0.5/boe for each 5% of krone strength (derived from management's FX attribution), and do not assume the USD 2.5bn capex cap buys the same volume each year.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3346th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro bites hardest at the company level, through three channels the company does not hedge: the commodity price, the krone and the tax translation. At sector level it bites through the discount rate and rotation.

  • Company level. All revenue is in USD, EUR and GBP; costs and tax are in NOK. A 10% NOK strengthening cut 2025 pre-tax profit by USD 544m (company disclosure). The functional-currency switch removed debt-translation noise but left tax payable revalued in NOK: a stronger krone now raises both dollar costs and reported tax expense at once (Q3 2026: 85% effective tax rate). Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley see the krone supported by energy prices and Norges Bank's 4.5% policy rate (held to June 2027; Nordea expects no cut before 2028).
  • Sector level. Higher rates raise the discount rate on dividend-valued stocks: Morgan Stanley values Var on a dividend discount model at a 9% cost of equity (bear case 9.5%); the US ten-year is above 5% and the ECB hiked to 2.5% in September 2026, with Morgan Stanley expecting another hike in December. The weekly environment note reads risk-off beneath an index at its high, with defensives leading.
  • Industry level. The energy shock lifts all producers; the energy and Norway tilts (Overweight) are conditional on Brent staying above USD 85, a level the latest weekly note could not test.
  • Skew. If the energy premium fades, the company suffers lower price and probably a stronger krone relative to its cost base only if Norges Bank holds; the more likely combined path is lower price with a still-firm krone, which is the worst combination for Var's margins.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–40

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the most material other dynamic is the sector-wide cash-tax trough, which makes H2 2026 to H1 2027 free cash flow look weak at the very moment earnings look strong; second is supplier and peer consolidation; third is investor positioning.

  • Cash-tax trough (negative for cash, timing). The producers delivering start-ups are at their cash trough in H2 2026 as peak spend and deferred taxes on the H1 windfall land together (research report, 2 November 2026). For Var, H2 2026 cash tax is about USD 2.84bn (USD 1.14bn paid in Q3, about USD 1.7bn guided for Q4), above the USD 2.5bn guided after Q2; the 2027 catch-up follows. Equinor expects NOK 93bn of Q4 shelf tax after NOK 61bn in Q3, confirming the pattern.
  • Consolidation (mixed). Producers: Var-BlueNord, DNO-Sval and DNO-Capricorn, Harbour-Wintershall Dea. Suppliers: Saipem and Subsea 7. The first gives Var scale and a Danish hub; the second raises supplier power.
  • Positioning and rotation (negative skew). European majors at 9.3x 2027 earnings against 14.2x in the United States; energy positioning at the 69th percentile; the weekly environment note's Momentum factor at Underweight and the energy tilt conditional on Brent above USD 85. Var's 24-month-forward P/E (11.5x) sits at the 87th percentile of five years and its trailing P/E (12.8x) at the 99th of ten, the highest in its cohort.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A821st of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a low threat of every type; entry barriers (capital, state pre-qualification, decommissioning liabilities, infrastructure access) are high and the only entrants are adjacent incumbents buying in, which supports asset prices rather than threatening Var.

  • Adjacent (other European E&Ps and integrated groups: ORLEN, DNO, Harbour): the main entry route, by acquisition; mostly a source of competition for assets and supplier capacity.
  • Standard (new licensees in APA rounds): 21 applicants in 2026, mostly incumbents.
  • Orthogonal (service companies or utilities moving upstream): judged highly unlikely by the business foundations report (contractors prefer alliance margins to asset and decommissioning risk).
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3043rd of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a source of risk, partly mitigated by early commitments; suppliers are capturing value from the upcycle before Var's projects deliver theirs.

  • Behaviour: high-specification rigs contracted well ahead of need (Transocean added about USD 1.1bn of Norwegian backlog with Equinor and Shell); subsea tender pipelines at records; contractors prefer integrated EPCI awards; equipment lead times long.
  • Var's mitigants: TechnipFMC five-year integrated collaboration (Ofelia, Gjøa Nord); Ocean Installer fast-track EPCI for Balder Next New Wells; Aker Solutions five-year frame agreement for Jotun and Balder modifications; Deepsea Bergen fixed for 2028-2031; COSL Prospector and Pioneer under charter; lease liabilities for rigs, helicopters and supply vessels USD 169.2m at September 2026.
  • Residual risk: dependence on concentrated specialist suppliers for critical subsea scope; no disclosure of vendor concentration, day-rate escalation or contract re-openers; history of yard-driven overruns (Balder X refurbishment at Rosenberg doubled to USD 4.3bn; Balder Future NOK 20.0bn to 48.5bn gross). The risk is more schedule slippage and cost creep than supplier failure.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0135C-0335C-0455C-0845C-3242C-3760C-4325
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3550th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the price signal is strong, but it is a supply shock with no demand cushion and less than two years' visibility, so it must be modelled as cyclical, not structural.

  • Gas (about 35% of Var's output, 32% in Q3 2026; rising towards 40% with BlueNord's 41-43% gas mix). European gas demand is in structural decline: 340 bcm in 2025, 16% below the 2017-2021 average; it fell 0.3% in the first eight months of 2026; euro-area industrial gas demand was down 8% year on year in September (France -14%, Germany -5%). The price is high because supply is short: storage about 74% full before winter (the lowest pre-winter level in Morgan Stanley's 15-year dataset), Gulf LNG disrupted by the Hormuz closure, Russian LNG banned from January 2027 and pipeline gas by late 2027. Against that, new LNG capacity (United States, Qatar) is expected to rebalance the market from 2028, and global LNG demand fell 5.4% in H1 2026.
  • Oil (about 65%). No structural demand decline (DNB Carnegie), but the International Energy Agency sees global demand down about 1 mb/d in 2026 with a 2 mb/d rebound in 2027; inventories are drawn (507m barrels cumulatively since February per Morgan Stanley), refinery cracks are strong, and high prices are already destroying some demand (Allianz). Brent reached USD 111 on 22 October 2026; Deutsche Bank expects normalisation from Q1 2027; 68% of investors surveyed expect Brent below USD 100 at end-2026.
  • What this means for the model. The change is cyclical and supply-led. Volumes Var can sell are not at risk (Norwegian pipeline gas is the security-of-supply barrel, and about 65% of gas is under long-term offtake contracts); price is. The visibility of today's price is one to two winters at most; the consensus deck (about USD 80/boe realised in 2027, about USD 69/boe in 2028, derived) already assumes normalisation. The skew over twelve months is two-way and wide: a cold winter with weak Gulf flows lifts gas sharply (Morgan Stanley winter TTF EUR 90/MWh), while a Hormuz reopening or mild winter takes TTF towards EUR 55/MWh (Morgan Stanley bear case) and Brent into surplus (Deutsche Bank).
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F3552nd of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: two disruption forces are strengthening and both act against Var's gas exposure over the 18-month-to-four-year horizon; technology is a disruption that helps every operator equally, so it is not an advantage for Var.

  • Energy transition on gas demand (strengthening, slow). Electrification, efficiency, renewables and heat pumps have cut European gas use 16% below its 2017-2021 average; Bernstein expects networks to shift towards flexibility and partial decommissioning by 2045. This lowers the terminal value of undeveloped gas resources and the willingness of buyers to sign long contracts.
  • LNG supply expansion (strengthening, dated 2027-2028). New liquefaction, led by the United States, is expected to rebalance global LNG from 2028; this is the specific force that removes the scarcity premium the 2026-2027 cash flows rest on.
  • Offshore technology (strengthening, cost-reducing, shared). Standardised subsea tie-backs and AI tools lower costs for all; Var reports selected subsea lead times cut from 30 to 18 months and Balder Next New Wells subsea delivery cost cut by 30%, but Equinor targets a 50% cost and lead-time cut and Aker BP has the clearer technology-to-earnings pathway. An expert estimates technology has cut supply-chain costs by at least 20%; the benefit is competed away unless Var converts it into better uptime than peers, which it has not yet done (91% efficiency against Equinor's about 92%).
  • Disproved: a structural collapse in oil demand within the horizon (DNB Carnegie finds few signs of it).
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C-5524th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the regime is supportive today and carries a dated binary test at end-2027; near-term policy changes are mostly favourable, and the downside is in the 2027-2028 fiscal and carbon decisions.

  • Supportive now. The 78% marginal tax with immediate expensing means the state carries most development cost; pre-2023 sanctioned projects get an 86.9% deduction; the state refunds 78% of exploration spend. Norway opened 70 additional blocks in the 2026 APA round (awards early 2027); Var received 14 licences in APA 2025, six as operator. The EU's phase-out of Russian gas (LNG from January 2027, pipeline by late/September 2027) raises the strategic value of Norwegian supply. The EU Methane Regulation was postponed by one year.
  • Dated tests. The temporary tax regime's uplift expires for projects submitted after end-2027, and the tax reform commission reports late 2027 (the memo calls it binary). This creates an incentive to sanction before end-2027, which supports management's eight-sanction 2027 plan, and a cliff after it. The carbon tax rises towards about NOK 2,400 per tonne by 2030 (Var's impairment testing assumes NOK 2,371 in real 2025 terms).
  • Litigation. Breidablikk (Var 34.4%): the Borgarting Court of Appeal confirmed the PDO invalidity in November 2025; the Ministry resolved to maintain the approvals in May 2026; the Supreme Court hearing was set for August 2026 and its outcome is not in the inputs. Activities have continued.
  • Arctic. The European Commission maintains its call for an Arctic drilling moratorium (21 September 2026); Norway says it alone decides, but the Barents (Johan Castberg, Goliat, Snøhvit) carries long-dated policy risk.
  • Denmark. The Danish North Sea Agreement phases out production by 2050; BlueNord's DUC concessions expire in 2042 unless extended.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F4550th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the sector's "guide low and beat" dynamic is accelerating Var's earnings revisions, not its physical demand; it is a strong near-term tailwind for estimates that is already priced and that turns into a headwind if spot falls towards the deck.

  • The sector digest's rule is that when planning prices sit far below realised prices, consensus is a floor. That has held through 2026: FY2027 EPS consensus is +22% over one month, +39% over three, +81% over twelve; FY2028 has now joined (+11% over one month), which partly fires the memo's own revisit trigger ("the FY2028 consensus EPS line rising with the nearer years would say the deck is wrong").
  • Var's own planning prices are conservative (impairment testing at a flat USD 65 real; Norne at USD 75 for 2027-2028; DNB Carnegie USD 75 Brent and USD 12/mmbtu gas from 2028).
  • The structural element is European energy security: Norway supplies about 30% of European gas, and the Russian phase-out raises the value of Norwegian molecules; this supports contract value and price volatility more than volume.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F4249th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: physical offtake is secure and price-insensitive in volume; it supports the thesis on volumes and not at all on price, which is the variable the thesis needs.

  • Buyers: Eni entities took about 79% of 2025 revenue and about USD 2.85bn of Q3 2026's USD 3.57bn; EU and UK utilities and industrials take the gas; about 65% of gas sold is under long-term offtake contracts with market-linked pricing and price-review clauses; the 12-year Eni contract covers up to 5 bcm.
  • Behaviour: European buyers are procuring for security (Germany instructed SEFE to buy and inject; storage refill to 90% by October 2027 needs LNG imports up 24% year on year per Redburn), while industrial consumption falls (France -15% in four weeks to early October). This is forced procurement at high prices, not growing consumption.
  • Fragility: low for volumes; high for price if storage refills and LNG flows normalise. Counterparty fragility: related-party receivables reached USD 1.14bn at Q3 2026; Eni is investment-grade.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C6019th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: helping now (licensing, fiscal support, EU security policy), with fragility concentrated in three dated decisions: the end-2027 tax review, carbon costs to 2030, and the courts on emission assessments.

  • Helps: 78% marginal tax with immediate expensing; 86.9% deduction for pre-2023 projects to end-2027; exploration refund; 2026 APA round with 70 new blocks; EU Russian gas phase-out; methane rules delayed.
  • Hurts: carbon tax rising towards NOK 2,371-2,400 per tonne by 2030; electrification expectations (Halten and Snorre power-from-shore discontinued as uneconomic; Snøhvit Future up 52% to NOK 20bn and delayed to H1 2029); EU Arctic moratorium push; Denmark's 2050 phase-out.
  • Fragility: the regime is politically durable (Morgan Stanley's Martijn Rats calls the 78% rate highly stable), but a modest change matters because it acts on a 78% marginal system (DNB Carnegie). The Breidablikk case shows courts can reopen approvals on downstream-emission grounds.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F2552nd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: for a price-taking producer the relevant "market" is its accessible resource base, and that base supports a plateau, not growth; mid-term volume growth is about zero after BlueNord, so revenue growth is the price.

  • Resources: about 2.4bn boe of 2P reserves and 2C resources pro forma, about 15 years of reserve and resource life, over 3bn boe of total potential with more than half undeveloped; 15 projects in execution with about 370 mmboe net 2P; about 30 early-phase projects targeting about 500 mmboe of 2C.
  • Constraints: base decline 8-9% a year; scheduled reserves and risked resources for 2026-2029 fell from 527 to 438 mmboe in nine months; the Ministry says remaining acreage is mature and discoveries are getting smaller; DUC concessions expire in 2042.
  • Demand-side "market": Norwegian gas meets about 30% of European demand, which is itself declining.
  • Mid-term growth allowed: the group plateau of about 450 kboepd is the ceiling; my base case is 430-440 kboepd for 2027-2028 (about 390 standalone plus 45 BlueNord, less decline not fully offset), with downside to DNB Carnegie's standalone 347 kboepd by 2028.

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0616C-1022
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F1653rd of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: revenue is at its cyclical peak in 2026 and consensus already has it turning down in 2027 and 2028; the volume engine has stopped growing and the price engine has peaked.

  • Volume. Year-on-year growth went from +51% (Q1 2026) to +31% (Q2) to about zero (Q3: 369 against 370 kboepd). The start-up wave (nine projects in 2025, Johan Castberg and Balder X) is complete; from here, standalone volume is a plateau maintained against 8-9% base decline. The only step-up ahead is BlueNord's about 45 kboepd from closing.
  • Price. Realised price rose from USD 76/boe (2024) to about USD 101-103/boe in Q2-Q3 2026, with Q4 supported by crude differentials of USD 6-10/bbl above Brent and winter gas.
  • Revenue. USD 8.1bn (FY2025, brokers' basis) to USD 14.4bn (FY2026, +77.5%), then -10.5% and -16.3% in FY2027 and FY2028 on consensus. The turn is not yet visible in reported numbers (Q3 revenue +68.6% year on year), but it is visible in the forward line and in the sequential trend (Q3 revenue -4% on Q2).
  • Model implication. 2026 is the base year from which every forward line falls; the Consumer should not annualise H2 2026.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F2252nd of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Thematic drivers, one by one (from the sector and industry digests):

  • Capital swing back to barrels (Big). Tailwind for basin activity and licensing; headwind for Var's costs because it fills supplier order books (rigs, subsea). Net: slightly negative for Var.
  • Balance sheets at record lows (Big). Tailwind: Var's 0.3x leverage and USD 7.5bn liquidity let it pay through a cash trough. Net: positive.
  • Guiding low and beating (Big). Tailwind for revisions while spot exceeds the deck; reverses if spot normalises. Net: positive for 6-12 months, then neutral.
  • The 2027 gas wave (Big, dated). Headwind for a producer whose gas share rises with BlueNord: the 2027-2028 earnings are exposed to the very LNG additions that end the scarcity. Net: negative.
  • Self-funded regional platforms (Big, early for North Sea independents). Tailwind for the narrative: BlueNord is exactly this story ("synergies stated and then beaten are the tell"; Neptune's synergies came in at USD 600m against USD 300m guided). Net: positive.
  • Nobody is building (industry). Var is a buyer of scarce capacity, not an owner: headwind.

Value chain, industry and sector together. At the value-chain level the headwinds dominate (supplier power, demand erosion, krone, carbon); at the industry level the tailwind dominates for now (price, storage deficit, Russian phase-out, revisions); at the sector level the balance is set by the discount rate and rotation, which currently lean against a high-multiple, dividend-valued name.

Overall picture. External forces are at peak tailwind and turning. The near-term (next six months) is supported by winter gas and crude differentials; the 12-to-24-month horizon carries a dated headwind cluster (LNG wave, Russian replacement completed, end-2027 fiscal test, cash-tax catch-up). The tailwinds are strong but cyclical and largely priced (DNB Carnegie: implied oil USD 105 against Brent USD 108.2 on 20 October); the headwinds are slower but structural.

Internal change forces/risks

F4752nd of 55

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Real, well-funded corporate change whose earnings payoff has been spent, now meeting a delivery test. The 2023-2025 change (new CEO, two big start-ups, Neptune, deleveraging, rising distributions) has propagated through every layer and been paid for by the market. The 2026 changes (BlueNord, hub swaps, project factory, higher dividends) extend reserve life and gas exposure and are well resourced, but they are per-share neutral on earnings and they come with a visible slippage in the measures that drive the operating model (efficiency 97% to 91%, unit cost back to USD 11, volumes at the bottom of guidance, four quarters of cash shortfalls). Management raises what it controls through capital (dividends, long-term targets, M&A) and under-delivers on what it controls through operations. For the model, this translates into: (1) dividend at USD 350m a quarter as a floor with the extraordinary as the flex; (2) volumes and costs set at the bottom of management's ranges, not the midpoint; (3) BlueNord as +45 kboepd, +248.4m shares, about USD 45m a year of post-tax synergies phasing in from 2027, and a modest rise in group unit cost.

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement24C-1432C-8634C-5944C-8135D-0338
Group judgement

Risks

F2450th of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is asymmetric to the downside because the price, the multiple and the narrative are all at or near their peaks while the growth engine has stopped. The fundamental risk with the largest magnitude, commodity normalisation, is not low-probability; the most plausible company-specific risk, hub outages, has just recurred; and the most asymmetric, a fiscal change, has a date (late 2027). Sentiment risks are unusually concentrated: Var has the highest valuation percentile in its cohort, a valuation that rests on a yield the forward cash flows only cover in 2026, and a visible bear case. A fair trough is about NOK 27-35, with a soft floor from the dividend if the USD 350m base holds. For the model, this means the downside scenario should combine USD 60-65 Brent with the H1 2027 cash-tax catch-up and a hub outage, and show net debt and dividend coverage explicitly rather than EPS alone.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are slipping on the operational measures that drive the model (uptime, unit cost, volume delivery), improving on safety and project-delivery tools, and unchanged on governance.

  • Slipping. Operated production efficiency 91% for nine months of 2026 against 97% in Q1 and 92% for 2025; Q3 outages at Johan Castberg (power generation), Jotun (gas compressor failure on restart) and Åsgard (curtailments), described as non-reservoir; unit cost raised by USD 1/boe; a fourth consecutive quarter of cash-flow shortfall against consensus. Management called the company "fully de-risked with major projects complete" in Q3 2025 and then suffered downtime in December 2025 and again in Q3 2026.

  • Cost discipline. Capex held flat despite weaker cash, which is discipline in form; Balder FPU retirement in 2028 should cut gross opex by about USD 130m a year.
  • Governance. No change beyond the Eni board rotation.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F4442nd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: partly congruent: goals, strategy and capital allocation are aligned around cash returns, but execution culture and technology adoption lag the strategy's "project factory" claims, and the ownership structure creates cultural friction.

  • Congruent: an explicit harvest strategy, a dividend-first capital framework, incentives tied to relative TSR, cost and ESG (20% of the annual variable pay), holding requirements, broad employee share ownership (86% of employees in the June 2026 saving programme), improving safety.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D3837th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly concentrated, and the answer has two halves that pull in opposite directions for predictability. Against the last three to five years, the whole difference is one driver, volume, and almost all of it is already in the run rate or contracted, which makes the level of the forecasts against history unusually secure. Against FY2026, the peak year from which every line falls, three-quarters of the EPS change is one driver the company neither controls nor hedges, the realised price; and the largest single driver of the free cash flow change is not the price at all but the timing of tax payments, which is mechanical and guided. The drivers the company does control (uptime, unit cost, capital spending) are diffuse and together explain a few percent of any difference.

Against history: one driver, already delivered. FY2027-FY2030 base-case revenue averages 11,480 against 7,385 for FY2023-FY2025, 55% more. Production averages about 424 against about 278 kboepd, 53% more; the realised price is about USD 74 against about USD 73 per boe (derived; FY2024 production taken at the middle of the 280-300 kboepd range it was guided and delivered in). So the forecasts do not rely on prices above recent history: the FY2028-FY2030 deck (Brent USD 72-73, TTF EUR 30-35) is the price of the three years before the shock. And the volume is not a forecast of growth: Var produced 406 kboepd in Q1 2026 and 383 for nine months; the base case asks for 382-385 standalone plus 43 from an acquisition that shareholders have approved. Margins follow from the same single fact: at a like-for-like price the EBITDA and EBIT margins are where they were (D-01, point 3).

Against FY2026: EPS from 0.798 to 0.339 in FY2028, a fall of 0.459 (derived).

Driver EPS effect Share of the fall Nature
Realised price, from about USD 100 to USD 71/boe on about 155 mmboe, after 78.5% tax -0.35 76% External, unhedged, two-way
FY2026 flattered below EBIT: about USD 0.2bn of currency gains in finance items and a 75.4% tax rate; neither repeats (finance items return to their run rate, tax to 78.5%) -0.08 17% Mechanical, already visible in Q3 2026
BlueNord per share: about +0.045 of earnings against -0.072 of dilution -0.03 6% Contracted
Standalone volume, unit cost, depreciation, coupons about 0 about 1% Company-controlled

Against FY2026: free cash flow after distributions from +2,165 to -845 in FY2028, a swing of about USD 3.0bn (derived). The swing in tax timing is USD 3.1bn (cash tax USD 2.0bn below the charge in FY2026, USD 1.1bn above it in FY2028). The after-tax effect of the lower price is USD 0.95bn. Working capital gives back about USD 0.8bn (a build in the rising year, a release after), and BlueNord adds USD 0.2-0.3bn. So the cash line that the narrative will be fought over is driven three times more by tax timing than by the price. Two consequences. First, cumulative cash is far more predictable than annual cash: FY2026-FY2028 free cash flow after distributions sums to about +USD 1.6bn on the base deck, although the single years are +2.2bn, +0.3bn and -0.8bn. Second, the "dividend not covered in FY2028" result is close to independent of the price path, because a higher price in 2027 is taxed in 2028 (D-04).

What is new against the last four to six years, and what is not. Not new: this has always been a one-factor earnings stream with a step from projects or acquisitions (revenue +62% in FY2022, -30% in FY2023; about 80% of production growth acquired). New, and all of it below EBIT: a tax charge that now moves with the krone (the tax payable of USD 3.3bn is revalued each quarter: 65% effective rate in Q2 2026, 85% in Q3); finance costs that are no longer capitalised (USD 220m was in 2025); two hybrids instead of one; 10% more shares. These four did not exist in the FY2021-FY2023 figures the forecasts are compared with, and they are why EPS grows less than volume per share. They are individually small, mechanical and forecastable, with the exception of the tax translation, which is random by quarter and nets towards zero over years.

Concentration elsewhere in the case, for completeness. The Balder area and Johan Castberg carry about 40% of output; Eni entities buy about 79% of sales (USD 2,852m of USD 3,566m in Q3 2026); one fiscal regime takes 78% at the margin. Each concentrates risk; none is a driver of the forecast differences.

What this means for predictability. Concentration helps when the dominant driver is knowable and hurts when it is not. Here the dominant driver of the level against history (volume) is knowable and largely banked; the dominant driver of the path from the peak (price) is the least knowable variable in the case; and the dominant driver of the cash path (tax timing) is knowable given the price. The forecasts are therefore easy to trace and hard to pin: one number, the realised price, explains most of any error, and the company's own actions explain almost none.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D5737th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: a genuine and well-resourced change of phase, from production build-out to harvesting, plus a first step outside Norway; it is clearly articulated but its success depends on repeatable small-project execution that the 2026 operating record has not yet proved.

  • From build to harvest. The earlier strategy doubled production via ExxonMobil Norway, Neptune and the big start-ups; the stated next phase is increasing recovery, accelerating tie-backs and extracting more value from existing infrastructure ("project factory", BEST initiative). Capex is held at about USD 2.5bn a year for 2027-2032 on the combined plan, with FCF neutrality claimed at USD 40-45 Brent.
  • Hub consolidation. Pandion (10% of Nova, 20% of Ofelia; USD 110m), the DNO swap (5% of Gjøa for 5% of Nova plus Ringhorne North), the Equinor swap (32.5% of Peon with operatorship, against 5% of Fram and Grosbeak), and divestment of 20% of Goliat and 25% of Fenja for up to USD 350m while keeping operatorship. These raise control over tie-back sequencing around Gjøa and Balder.
  • Geography. BlueNord adds a fifth hub (36.8% non-operated interest in the DUC, operated by TotalEnergies): longevity beyond 2040 and more gas, at the cost of a new operator and regime.
  • Resourcing. USD 7.5bn of liquidity, 0.3x leverage, 15 projects in execution (about 370 mmboe net 2P), about 30 early-phase projects (about 500 mmboe 2C), 10-15 exploration wells a year, secured rigs.
  • Weakness. Scheduled reserves and risked resources for 2026-2029 fell from 527 mmboe (December 2025) to 438 mmboe (September 2026); the project factory has quantified wins on selected projects only.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F4047th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or latent?

Judgement: Var has no pricing power; the 2026 price rise is a nominal commodity windfall, and its commercial premiums are small, real but partly given back by its contract structure.

  • Realised price rose from USD 76/boe (2024) to about USD 102/boe (Q3 2026) because Brent and TTF rose, not because of anything Var did.
  • Real but small commercial gains: crude sold at a USD 6/bbl premium to dated Brent in Q2 and Q3 2026, with USD 6-10/bbl expected in Q4 (some cargoes up to USD 20 above Brent per Norne); gas marketing is said to generate about USD 1.3bn above spot over two-year cycles (Norne).
  • Given back: Q3 2026 gas realised about USD 18/boe below spot because 29% of volumes were fixed or year-ahead (about USD 87/boe) and 25% month-ahead indexation lagged a rising market by about USD 22/boe; about 30% of Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 gas is fixed at about USD 82/boe. Aker BP realised 6% more than Var in Q1 2026.
  • For the model: price realisation should be modelled as a differential to benchmarks (crude +USD 3-6/bbl, gas a lagged, partly fixed basket), not as a lever.

Management claims accretion to production, reserves, CFFO and FCF per share but has given no EPS bridge.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

B6011th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns are being stepped up deliberately and ahead of earnings, using a record-strong balance sheet as the bridge; the step-up is real, the sustainability depends on the deck.

  • Quarterly dividend: USD 300m (Q1 2026), 350m (Q2), 400m (Q3, including USD 50m extraordinary), 400m guided for Q4; 2026 total USD 1.45bn against USD 1.2bn in 2025; 18 consecutive stable or rising quarterly distributions to Q2 2026, over USD 4.4bn since the February 2022 IPO.

  • Model judgement. The USD 350m base is a floor management will defend with debt; the USD 50m extraordinary is the flex. Policy (25-30% of post-tax CFFO through the cycle) would imply about USD 1.1-1.3bn a year on consensus 2027-2028 CFFO (USD 4.45bn and 4.25bn), i.e. below the USD 1.4bn floor (derived), so policy and practice diverge from 2027 and the gap is funded by net debt.

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: broad and well-resourced in capital terms, but mature and increasingly offset by operational slippage; the change that drove the past three years' re-rating has propagated fully, and the new changes are mostly about longevity, not earnings growth.

  • Strong and broad: strategy phase-change, hub consolidation, BlueNord, project factory, raised distributions, record balance sheet, disciplined use of expensive equity as acquisition currency, credible CEO with an integration record.
  • Resourced: USD 7.5bn liquidity, secured rigs and suppliers, a 15-project pipeline at about USD 30/boe breakeven and about 35% IRR.
  • Offsets: volume and cost delivery at or below the bottom of guidance; efficiency down; cash conversion misses; reserve schedule down; per-share earnings neutral from BlueNord.
  • For the model: corporate change supports the plateau and the dividend; it does not supply EPS growth. The company-driven upgrade to the EPS trajectory is behind us; the next 18 months are a test of delivery against already-raised ambitions.

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

  • First call date of the EUR 750m 7.862% hybrid (February 2029): refinancing at a lower coupon would add modestly to EPS.
  • Danish DUC licence extension decision (2042 to 2050; timing not stated).
  • Novelty: low to medium; foundation: dependent on execution.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F4244th of 54

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F4244th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

A good neighbourhood for access and funding, a poor one for margins, and Var is a mid-ranked house in it. The basin is concentrated, cooperative and protected by high entry barriers and a fiscal regime in which the state carries most development cost; that justifies stable volumes, low cost of debt and continued licence access in the model. But the price is set outside the basin and is currently a geopolitical premium, suppliers hold cyclical pricing power, and long-run European gas demand is declining, so none of the structure's support reaches the margin line. Within the structure, Var consolidates (BlueNord, hub swaps) without being the most advantaged operator: it is third on cost, behind on uptime and technology, and dependent on Eni for about 79% of its offtake. Historical sector patterns say Var has already harvested the volume step and the corporate-change re-rating; what remains is the "consensus is a floor" tailwind while spot exceeds the deck, set against the late-cycle pattern of peak volume, peak price and peak multiple. For the model, the industry analysis argues for flat-to-slightly-rising unit costs, no price premium beyond small crude differentials, and a valuation that should converge towards peers whose cash yields are rising as Var's fall.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D5534th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: helping, with low fragility, except for two single points of failure: the third-party gas transport and processing system, and the concentration of offtake and credit on Eni.

  • Gas moves through the state-linked Gassled/Gassco system to exit points in the UK, Germany and France; Var books capacity under financial removal provisions; BlueNord adds Nybro and Den Helder via three Ørsted pipelines. Unplanned Gassco or processing outages (Kårstø, Kollsnes) during winter are the "highest-plausibility" failure pathway in the risks report.
  • Crude is sold one to two months before delivery, which creates realisation lags in volatile markets (Q4 2026 benefits from September's stronger differentials).
  • Shipping for the whole oil portfolio is secured beyond 2030 at about USD 2-3/bbl.
  • Working-capital tools: receivables purchase agreements derecognised USD 421m at end-2025; USD 325.6m sold in mid-2026. These pull cash forward and make reported working capital less informative.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4235th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors mildly hurt Var by bidding for the same scarce inputs and by offering investors a cleaner version of the same exposure; Var's position is not fragile in its operations but is fragile in investor preference as peers' cash flows inflect.

  • Aker BP: lowest-cost operator after Equinor, alliance model (Skarv Satellites ten months early, Symra nine months early), 70-80% NOK hedging, 525 kboepd target by 2028; its FCF yield is expected to rise to about 14% in 2027 and about 20% in 2028 as capex falls 56% (Bernstein), just as Var's falls (consensus FCF USD 1.45bn in 2027, USD 0.95bn in 2028; Var's capex/revenue rises from 19% to 28%, the peer table).
  • Equinor: dominant infrastructure owner, 6-8 tie-backs a year from its NCS 2035 model; competes for the same suppliers; DNB Carnegie judges Equinor the better long-term risk-reward with lower downside.
  • DNO and BlueNord (until closing): far cheaper (DNO P/E 5.8x; P/NAV 0.63-0.65x; 2026e FCF yield about 32-33%).
  • The fragile point: a reader comparing 2027-2028 cash yields will prefer Aker BP's rising profile to Var's falling one at a lower multiple percentile (Aker BP's P/E 11.3x is at its 43rd percentile, Var's 12.8x at its 99th).

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F3548th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption acts on Var through its customers (falling gas demand), its competitors in supply (LNG), its regulators (carbon and electrification) and its suppliers (consolidation and scarcity); together they leave the near-term position robust and the long-run gas value fragile.

  • Customers: efficiency and electrification reduce European gas consumption; buyers may resist long-dated contracts as networks are repurposed by 2045 (Bernstein).
  • Competing supply: US and Qatari LNG growth could weaken Norwegian pipeline-gas pricing power later in the decade (Oxford Institute expert discussion), though Norway keeps a delivery-cost and reliability advantage.
  • Regulators: carbon pricing and electrification raise operating and capital costs of mature hubs; carbon removals for residual emissions (NOK 250-300m a year in earlier analysis) are an under-disclosed future cash cost.
  • Suppliers: technology lowers industry costs (benefits all), while consolidation (three subsea production-system vendors; Saipem-Subsea 7) raises supplier pricing power.
  • The company itself: Var is a pure-play upstream producer with no renewables or CCS revenue stream (the Trudvang CCS licence is an option, with concept selection targeted for 2026 and sanction for 2027 if commercial terms materialise), so it cannot offset transition risk internally.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F5041st of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a clear number three on the Norwegian shelf today, becoming a close number two among shelf independents (and arguably Europe's largest independent by its own definition) after BlueNord; it is about a fifth of the number one and roughly level with Aker BP, which is the relevant rival.

  • Equinor: 1,410-1,446 kboepd on the shelf (operator of about 70% of shelf output; group equity production 2,165-2,313 kboepd). C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5531st of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the producing basin is highly concentrated (top five far above 30%, and far more than five times numbers six to ten), but the market that sets Var's prices, global oil and European gas, is not, so concentration protects access and cooperation, not margins.

  • Shelf: active companies fell from 56 (2013) to 25 (2024); Equinor, Aker BP and Var together produce roughly 2.2 mboe/d of about 4 mboe/d (derived from the production figures above); the tier below (OKEA 26.6 kboepd; DNO about 85; Harbour Norway about 180) is far smaller.
  • Price-setting markets: oil is set globally with OPEC+ as the swing supplier; European gas is set at TTF, where Norway supplies about 30% of European gas and Equinor markets the state's SDFI volumes as one unit, but LNG supply from the United States and Qatar sets the marginal price.
  • The UK shelf has consolidated to four dominant operators (NEO NEXT+, Ithaca, Adura, Harbour); Denmark's DUC accounts for 85% of Danish oil and gas.

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B7210th of 54

Judgement: shares are stable at the top and move mainly by acquisition among incumbents; new entrants have taken negligible share.

Roughly 80% of Var's production growth was acquired rather than discovered (leadership digest).

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5729th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest and towards the most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: share consolidates towards scale; within scale it accrues to the lowest-cost, best-executing operators (Equinor, Aker BP). Var is among the consolidators but not among the most advantaged.

  • Mechanism: in an expensive, mature basin, scale is needed to operate hubs, to fund tie-backs and to win supplier capacity; sellers (majors exiting, private equity, distressed tier-two) keep supplying assets.
  • Advantage ranking on the shelf: unit cost Equinor about USD 6/boe, Aker BP USD 7.3 (2025; 8.8 in seasonal Q2 2026), Var USD 10.8-11.1; ROIC Var 13% against Aker BP 18%; realised price Aker BP 6% above Var (Q1 2026); emissions intensity Aker BP below 3 kg CO2e/boe, Equinor 6.3, Var 8.5-8.9.
  • Var's distinctive advantage is hub density plus an investment-grade balance sheet and a currency (its equity) the market currently values highly: it can buy, but it has not shown it runs assets better than the sellers.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F4045th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on the shelf, competitors are returns-first and cooperative; the price-setters outside the shelf (OPEC+ and, at the margin, US LNG and shale) are the actors whose volume decisions set Var's price.

  • Shelf peers screen projects on breakevens below USD 30-35/boe and IRR above 25% (Var), payback (Equinor 2.5 years), progressive or capped payouts; they co-explore and swap licences; the fiscal regime (state carries 78%) encourages investment but not price competition.
  • Prices do not fall when shelf volume falls; they respond to OPEC+ supply decisions, Middle East disruption and LNG additions. The relevant "attitude" risk is OPEC+ choosing volume (Bernstein: 2026 is the peak year of non-OPEC supply growth) or a Hormuz reopening flooding the market (Deutsche Bank: a large surplus).
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D3839th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years? Would competitors launch a "shark attack" on pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: shelf peers' cooperative attitude is stable; the global price-setters are unstable and situation-dependent, and a price collapse is a geopolitical or OPEC+ event, not a competitive attack on Var.

  • Shelf: cooperation has deepened (2026 exploration alliance; Equinor-Aker BP-Var swaps); no peer has any ability or reason to undercut a commodity price.
  • Global: OPEC+ behaviour has historically flipped between defending price and defending share; the 2026 shock is geopolitical (Hormuz), and its reversal ("oil dipping on peace hopes", undated, in the 6 November weekly note) is the closest analogue to a "shark attack" for Var: sudden, not company-specific, and capable of removing USD 20-30/bbl.
  • Suppliers are the competitors whose attitude has changed most: contractors now price for scarcity (rig day rates up, Subsea 7 margin target about 24%, possible Saipem-Subsea 7 merger).
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F4445th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: structurally supportive at the basin level and fragile at the price and supplier levels; support is durable for access and cost of capital, and fragile for margins.

  • Supportive: consolidated, cooperative peers; high entry barriers; a fiscal regime in which the state co-invests; pipeline infrastructure linked directly to Europe; Europe's need for Norwegian gas during the Russian phase-out.
  • Fragile: the price is set outside the basin and is currently a geopolitical premium; supplier power is at a cyclical high; the fiscal regime faces a dated review (end-2027); long-run European gas demand is declining.
  • For the model: the structure justifies modelling stable volumes, low credit costs and continued access to licences; it does not justify modelling price or margin persistence.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4049th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a medium and rising long-term threat for gas, a low near-term threat for oil; substitutes erode the terminal value of Var's gas resources more than they threaten 2027-2028 volumes.

  • Gas: renewables plus storage and grids, heat pumps, efficiency, industrial closures and fuel switching have cut European demand 16% below the 2017-2021 average; gas is below one-fifth of European electricity generation and acts as a balancing fuel; LNG is a substitute source of supply (and the medium-term price threat); experts see small modular reactors as a long-term threat and hydrogen as unlikely before 2050.
  • Oil: electric vehicles erode demand slowly; no structural decline within the horizon.
  • Nordic data-centre electricity demand (+40-50% by 2030) is a modest offsetting support for gas-fired flexibility.

Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F2454th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Var's advantages are mostly the basin's, not its own, and its own are positional (hubs) rather than operational. The Norwegian fiscal regime, consolidated cooperative peers and pipeline access to Europe are strong and shared; Var's specific edge is hub incumbency, which keeps tie-back economics attractive (about USD 30/boe breakeven, about 35% IRR) and is widening slowly through consolidation. Against that, Var is a price taker with no pricing power, third on cost, behind on uptime, technology and emissions intensity, and running on a treadmill whose base declines 8-9% a year. Nothing in its competitive position lifts its financial outputs relative to peers period by period; the reinvestment needed simply holds the plateau. For the model: assume no relative price premium beyond small crude differentials, unit costs that do not converge on Aker BP's, depreciation per barrel stays elevated (about USD 22-24/boe on consensus D&A of USD 3.3-3.5bn), and a reinvestment requirement of about USD 2.5bn a year just to hold volumes.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F4049th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Var has strong advantages that it shares with every shelf producer and only modest advantages of its own; its own advantages (hub positions) are widening slowly, while its relative cost position is narrowing against the leaders.

  • Regulatory and fiscal (shared, strong, partly time-limited). The state carries about 78% of development cost; 86.9% for pre-2023 projects; 78% exploration refund. It protects returns in a downturn and makes overruns cheap after tax, but it is not Var's: Aker BP and Equinor have it too. The 86.9% uplift ends for projects submitted after end-2027.
  • Hub incumbency and infrastructure access (production-related, moderate, widening). Participation in about half of producing shelf assets, five operated or core hubs after BlueNord, and hub consolidation (Gjøa with Nova, Ofelia and Peon; Balder with Ringhorne North) make infrastructure-led tie-backs cheap (sanctioned projects at about USD 30/boe breakeven and about 35% IRR; Balder Phase VI below USD 30 with payback under a year). This is the most company-specific advantage.
  • Route to European buyers (customer-related, moderate, slowly eroding). Pipeline delivery and long-term contracts make physical switching slow; LNG and floating regasification give buyers alternatives over time.
  • Commercial skill (weak to moderate). Crude premiums of USD 6/bbl and gas marketing worth about USD 1.3bn above spot over two-year cycles (Norne); but Q3 2026 gas realised USD 18/boe below spot, and Aker BP realised 6% more in Q1 2026.
  • Financial (temporary). Investment-grade ratings (BBB S&P, Baa3 Moody's, BBB Fitch), USD 7.5bn liquidity, and an equity valued at a premium that it can use as acquisition currency.
  • Disadvantages. Unit cost about USD 11/boe against Aker BP USD 7.3-8.8 and Equinor about 6; ROIC 13% against Aker BP 18%; operates only about a third of its licences; emissions intensity 8.5-8.9 kg CO2/boe against Aker BP below 3 and Equinor 6.3; experts describe slower technology adoption.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D5033rd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: large enough to matter (investment grade, supplier priority, a multi-project pipeline, acquisition capacity) but not large enough, or run well enough, to confer a cost advantage.

  • Var: about 390 kboepd in 2026, about 435-450 after BlueNord; consensus revenue USD 14.4bn and EBITDA USD 12.1bn for 2026; market value about USD 13.0bn today (2,496.4m shares at USD 5.20), about USD 14.3bn on the post-merger count (derived).

What it does not confer: lower unit cost or better uptime, which Aker BP achieves at a similar size through its alliance model and hub-level operations.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F4551st of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and not leading: Var sells an undifferentiated commodity whose Norwegian origin gives buyers security and proximity, but whose carbon intensity is worse than the shelf leaders'.

  • Utility versus alternatives: Norwegian pipeline gas is the primary alternative to Russian imports; it is reliable, close and lower-emission than most LNG; Var's methane intensity (0.02-0.03% of exported gas) is well below benchmarks.
  • Versus Norwegian peers: identical molecules through the same pipes; Var's upstream Scope 1 intensity of 8.5-8.9 kg CO2/boe is above Aker BP (below 3) and Equinor (6.3), which matters to buyers reporting under carbon-border rules (Equinor's Eneco deal transfers guarantees of origin).
  • Service quality: Var offers flexible month-ahead and day-ahead nominations under long-term contracts (Morgan Stanley); this is industry-standard rather than superior.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F1054th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: none, by the nature of the business; the evidence of application is limited to small, market-dependent premiums.

  • Realised prices track Brent and TTF; the 2026 rise is a windfall. Crude premiums (USD 6/bbl in Q2 and Q3 2026; USD 6-10/bbl expected in Q4) depend on North Sea differentials that management itself does not treat as structural.
  • Contract structure transfers some pricing power to customers in rising markets (Q3 2026 gas USD 18/boe below spot) and back in falling ones (month-ahead indexation reverses when hubs fall).
  • For the model: price is an input, not a lever; the only "pricing" decision is the hedge and fixed-price share (about 30% of near-term gas; collars on 13% of remaining 2026 post-tax oil with floors near USD 75/bbl).
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F3038th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: not applicable in a meaningful sense; the beneficiaries of Var's scale are the Norwegian state (through the 78% tax) and its shareholders (through distributions), not its customers, who pay market prices.

  • There is no mechanism by which lower unit cost reaches buyers; commodity prices are set at the margin by others.
  • The nearest analogue is the state's co-investment: the fiscal regime "shares" the cost of scale with the operator, which is why Norwegian producers invest through the cycle.
  • This is neither a strength nor a weakness relative to peers; it simply means there is no self-reinforcing customer loop to model.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F1853rd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: gravity works against Var; each year the base declines faster than the basin norm and depreciation per barrel rises, so outputs hold only through repeated heroic reinvestment. It is a treadmill business with a few useful mechanical tailwinds.

  • Against (mechanical). Natural decline of 8-9% a year (against a 4-6% typical mature North Sea hub average) means about 30-35 kboepd of standalone capacity must be replaced each year (derived from about 390 kboepd); depreciation rose from about USD 1.6bn (2023) to USD 3.0bn (2025) as the expensive, overrun-laden new capacity (Balder X, Johan Castberg) entered the base; carbon tax rises to 2030; Balder wells are "peaky" and decline quickly (management).
  • For (mechanical). The fiscal regime underwrites every tie-back; each hub consolidation makes the next infrastructure-led tie-back cheaper; the Balder FPU retirement in 2028 steps gross opex down by about USD 130m a year; scale lets the project factory reuse designs (subsea lead time 30 to 18 months on selected projects).
  • Heroic effort required. More than 50 new wells in 2026 (34 online by Q3), four sanctions to Q3 2026 with four more targeted by year-end, about eight in 2027, 10-15 exploration wells a year, and more than 100% organic reserve replacement targeted for 2027 (the 2025 2P replacement was 185%, with a four-year ratio above 160%).
  • Against peers: Aker BP's large projects (Yggdrasil, Valhall PWP-Fenris) add a step change in 2027-2028 that makes its outputs rise mechanically for several years; Var's do not.
4b

Management quality

F4444th of 5412 answers
Judgement44C-5555C-5646C-5757C-57b48C-5852C-5944C-6062C-6155C-6238C-6332C-6448
Group judgement

Company - Management

F4444th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

A capable deal-making CEO and a dividend-first system controlled by Eni; management can be relied on to raise distributions and do sensible transactions, and should not be relied on to beat operational guidance. Walker's record on capital and transactions is strong (Neptune synergies doubled, BlueNord bought with expensive paper, leverage to 0.3x, distributions raised 18 quarters running), and his personal alignment is real. But the operating record under him is bottom-of-range on volumes and above-target on costs, the team is competent rather than outlier, the culture shows friction and lags peers in technology, and governance is the legal minimum under a controlling parent that takes about 79% of output and needs cash. Communication is specific on inputs and silent on per-share outcomes. For the model, management quality translates into: volume and cost assumptions at the bottom of guidance; a dividend that is defended with debt; no buybacks; and a modest discount for governance in any valuation cross-check.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5530th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: big in scale and largely bought rather than built: the ambition is to be Europe's largest independent producer at about 450 kboepd for longer, not to grow organically, which puts it below Aker BP's organic ambition and above Equinor's.

  • Stated: about 450 kboepd long-term with BlueNord (from above 400, and 350-400 before that); production beyond 2040 from the Danish assets; about eight sanctions and more than 100% organic reserve replacement in 2027; "safest operator on the shelf"; net-zero Scope 1 and 2 operational emissions by 2030; a technology and AI programme (BEST).

  • Character: ambition expressed through consolidation and capital returns (dividends raised four times in 2026), consistent with Walker's Lundin record (built, then sold at a sector-high USD 20.4/boe EV on 2P).
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F4650th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: a sensible, coherent harvest strategy for a mature basin with real optionality, but not a moat-widening one: it maintains the plateau and the dividend, it does not fix the cost gap or the uptime gap, and it leaves the company unhedged on its biggest controllable financial risk (the krone).

  • Right: infrastructure-led tie-backs at about USD 30/boe breakeven; hub consolidation that raises control over sequencing; buying long-life Danish gas with expensive equity; flexible capex (about 70% of long-term capex uncommitted) that can be cut in a downturn; FCF neutrality claimed at USD 40-45 Brent; investment-grade discipline.
  • Bold: BlueNord is the first step outside Norway; the exploration alliance with Equinor and Aker BP.
  • Missing: an operating-model change of Aker BP's alliance kind (Var's project factory is narrower); a currency hedging policy (Aker BP hedges 70-80% of NOK spend); a per-share capital-allocation framework (no buybacks, no EPS bridge for BlueNord).
  • Dynamic across environments: in high prices it pays out more (extraordinary dividend); in low prices it can cut uncommitted capex and lean on the balance sheet. That is resilient, but it trades future volume for current dividends.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F3249th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: shareholders come first in cash, because the controlling shareholder needs cash; minority shareholders do not come first in governance, and the governance scaffolding is the legal minimum rather than prudent excess. The evidence points to "dividend first", not "per-share value first".

  • For shareholders. Distributions above earnings for years (payout ratios of 94% to 358% of Viewforth EPS in 2023-2025); management buys shares; executives must hold 50-100% of base salary in shares within five years; three-year lock-in on long-term incentive shares; clawback and malus; a relative-TSR modifier (Var ranked second of eleven for 2024, a 1.4 modifier); clean audits with no material weaknesses; an independent internal audit reporting to the audit committee.
  • Against minority shareholders. Eni holds 63.04% (57.33% after BlueNord) and appoints four of twelve directors through Class B shares; board independence about 33%; Eni entities bought about 79% of 2025 sales and hold USD 1.14bn of receivables; annual decommissioning fees to Eni with an NPV of USD 297m; CEO pay of NOK 37.1m against USD 1.73m at Aker BP and USD 2.21m at Equinor; board replacements announced without reasons; no evidence the board challenged the higher dividend, the guidance cut or BlueNord's capital allocation.
  • Prudence. Hybrids classified as equity flatter reported leverage (0.4x reported against 0.7x adjusted at Q2 2026, a "75% leverage understatement" in the risks report); receivables factoring pulls cash forward; the dividend was raised in the same release as a share-funded deal and a capex rise. Protections: Norwegian minority-oppression law and a 1% non-Eni vote requirement on key resolutions.
  • Why. Eni's interest is cash from a satellite it controls and sells into; the structure therefore reliably delivers distributions and reliably avoids buybacks or anything that would reduce Eni's control.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D4436th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

High margins, low durability, poor historical cash conversion, and a balance sheet that has always been the bridge between cash flow and the dividend. Pre-tax margins are among the best in the sector (EBITDA 79-84%), but after a 78% marginal tax, rising depreciation per barrel (about USD 22-25/boe) and hybrid coupons, the earnings base is thin, noisy and price-dominated: each USD 10/boe of price is worth about USD 0.12-0.13 of EPS, against about USD 0.03 for 20 kboepd of volume and about USD 0.013 for USD 1/boe of cost (all derived). EPS rises 156% in 2026 and consensus then takes back 25% and 32%; at a constant price, per-share earnings would be roughly flat from 2027 because BlueNord's volume is matched by its 9.95% dilution. Cash conversion was negative in most of 2019-2025 while dividends of about USD 7.3bn were paid; 2026 is the first year of true coverage, and coverage returns to about one times in 2027 and below one in 2028 on consensus. Financial leverage is comfortable on paper and the ordinary equity is close to nil. For the model: build price, volume, cost, depreciation, tax (with FX noise) and the cash-tax lag as separate drivers; treat the dividend as a fixed USD 350m-a-quarter floor; and let net debt absorb the difference.

Judgement: spectacular in 2026 and not durable: EPS growth is a commodity-and-volume spike that consensus reverses in 2027 and 2028, and BlueNord's 9.95% dilution means per-share growth will lag aggregate growth.

  • Opportunity set: 15 projects in execution (about 370 mmboe net 2P, about USD 30/boe breakeven, about 35% IRR); about 30 early-phase projects (about 500 mmboe 2C, about USD 35/boe breakeven, IRR above 25%); King (breakeven below USD 15/boe); Balder Phase VI (about 17 mmboe gross, payback under a year); exploration at about 45% success with over 70% of discoveries moving to development.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F3341st of 54

  • FactSet free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) against reported net income: -USD 473m (2023), -595m (2024), +550m (2025, 0.7x); consensus 1.7x (2026), 0.9x (2027), 0.9x (2028).   • For the model: forecast cash tax explicitly with a lag (I suggest the Consumer seek the instalment schedule, see requests), treat factoring as a constant, and expect working capital to move with price.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C-4225th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: noisy rather than aggressive; the noise comes from currency (in finance and, since June 2026, in tax), and the leakages that matter for EPS are hybrid coupons and an effective tax rate that can swing by seven points either side of 78%.

  • Basis gap: FY2025 EPS was USD 0.288 reported, 0.310 on the brokers' basis, 0.152 on FactSet's adjusted diluted basis and 0.145 on the Viewforth definition (net income USD 361m against USD 780m reported); the difference is mainly the USD 432m net exchange gain of 2025 (after a USD 370m loss in 2024, an USD 802m swing).

  • Sensitivity worth carrying in the model: one percentage point of effective tax rate on 2027 consensus pre-tax profit (about USD 6.9-7.1bn) is about USD 70m, or about USD 0.025 a share, roughly twice the effect of USD 1/boe of unit cost (derived).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C-6027th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: comfortable on every reported measure and modest as an EPS amplifier; the real leverage is the fixed dividend commitment set against a variable, tax-lagged cash flow, sitting on a balance sheet with almost no ordinary equity.

BlueNord adds its debt (RBL USD 725m, BNOR18 USD 400m at 7.875%), to be refinanced at Var's lower cost.
