How the items net out (each from the answers above).

  • Operating leverage (P-01): on a falling price it amplifies the decline; it is the reason EBIT falls nearly twice as fast as revenue from FY2026 to FY2028.
  • Financial leverage (P-02) and below-EBIT items (P-08): together about USD 0.08 a share a year of drag that did not exist in FY2022-FY2024 (capitalised interest, one hybrid), plus 10% dilution.
  • Cost efficiency (P-03): about USD 0.02-0.03 a share by FY2029, broadly offset by carbon tax and wage inflation; no net lift to margins.
  • Price (P-04): the dominant negative from Q2 2027; realised price falls about 29% from FY2026 to FY2028.
  • Loops (P-05): the tax lag moves cash from FY2027-FY2028 into FY2026; the trough in free cash flow after distributions is FY2028.
  • Track record (P-06): leans volumes and cost slightly against the company; already in the base.

The line that changes character most is free cash flow. History: negative or thin in most years, never covering the dividend over a cycle. Forecast: positive every year on my deck and covering dividends plus coupons in steady state at about USD 75 Brent and EUR 32-35 TTF (derived: free cash flow after distributions is about -USD 0.1-0.15bn a year at USD 72-73 Brent in FY2029-FY2030, and each USD 10/boe is worth about USD 0.34bn a year of post-tax cash once the lag clears). The company's claim of free cash flow neutrality at USD 40-45 Brent is consistent with my model before dividends.

Forecasts and upgrades

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F3044th of 54

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect runs the wrong way for an investment case: the favourable dynamics are fully reflected, and the dynamics that are not reflected are mostly unfavourable.

  • Guidance (I-01). It is not conservative and will not be the source of upgrades. The likely path is a 2027 production guide a few percent above our base case that is trimmed during the year, costs and capex met at the top, and payouts and long-dated ambitions held or raised. The single favourable gap is the dividend framework on 9 February 2027, where the policy arithmetic and Eni's needs make USD 400m a quarter or a special more likely than not; our base case has that only for one quarter.
  • Consensus (I-02). On operating lines our base case is at or a little above the street for FY2027, so there is no hidden operating shortfall. On EPS it is 14% and 17% below for FY2027 and FY2028, for reasons that are mechanical and dated, so the direction of EPS revisions from early 2027 is down even if the business does exactly what we expect. A year of estimate momentum (FY2027 EPS +81%) is ending as ratings drift lower.
  • Narrative (I-03). Both the bull and the bear case are well articulated above EBITDA; what is missing is the per-share arithmetic, the true multiple and the path-independence of the 2028 cash gap. Each of these, when it is noticed, weighs on the shares.

By horizon. In the next six months there are two things that could surprise positively against what is reflected: a cold winter with gas above the brokers' decks, and the February dividend framework. Both would support the price without raising earnings two years out. In months 6 to 18 the items not yet reflected are all on one side: consensus EPS re-based down; revenue below the prior year in every quarter from Q2 2027; a probable in-year trim of production guidance; first consolidated accounts showing higher depreciation and finance costs; the FY2028 cover debate becoming concrete as the Q2 2027 tax payment lands; the tax commission and the end of the temporary tax rules late in 2027. Beyond 18 months, the LNG additions and the thin, too-high FY2029 consensus point the same way.

What this means for the memo's own judgements.

  • Where the stock is in its life. The change that drove the re-rating (the doubling of volume, the first uncovered-to-covered dividend year, a year of rising estimates) is complete; estimate momentum is rolling over, ratings are being cut on valuation, and the multiple is at its peak for passive reasons (D-07). On the memo's scale this is a case that has been reinforcing and is now stalling, with the technical picture not yet confirming it (price above a rising 200-day average, relative strength at the 88th percentile).
  • The return at the guideline multiple. -24.3% to the 18-month column and -18.4% to the three-year column as the model stands; about -26% and -20% after my input corrections (my estimate). At the 18-month column the parts are +13.2% from dividends, -29.8% from the multiple and -10.8% from earnings as the model stands, and about +13%, -34% and -9% after my corrections (which lower today's earnings by more than the later years', and so appear as a higher starting multiple). The multiple part is the removal of Var's own excess over a sector that is itself expensive (9.41x is where Var would stand at the median peer's percentile); the earnings part is the price deck and the share count; the dividend is the only positive and is the part the company controls. A reader who believes the sector's re-rating will also fade should use a lower multiple than the guideline's.
  • What would have to be true for the gap to be favourable instead. Gas and oil prices holding near late-2026 levels through 2027, which would lift operating estimates by more than the re-basing takes away; and Var delivering the top half of a 440 kboepd guide at USD 10-11 a barrel, which its record does not support. The first is a view on the commodity that can be expressed through cheaper, better-operated vehicles with rising cash flow; the second is the capability the company has yet to show (D-02).

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

D4535th of 54

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: the pattern of the last three years is the best forecast of the next 18 months, because nothing that produced it has changed. Guidance on what management sets by decision (dividends, synergies, long-dated ambitions) is more likely to be raised than cut; guidance on spending and tax will be met, at the top of ranges; and in-year production guidance is more likely to be trimmed than met in the middle, with unit cost following it. So guidance events will not be a source of earnings upgrades. The one guidance event that can help the shares is the dividend framework on 9 February 2027, where the company's own policy arithmetic leaves room for more than our base case assumes.

The events. Q4 2026 trading update (late January 2027); FY2026 results with the first combined 2027 guidance (9 February 2027); Q1, Q2 and Q3 2027 results (late April, July, October 2027), each preceded by a trading update; FY2027 results with 2028 guidance (February 2028); Q1 2028 results (April 2028). A capital markets update has accompanied or followed the full-year results in each of the last two years.

Dividend, 2027 USD 350m base reaffirmed (95); Q1 2027 at USD 400m or more (60); an added special with the FY2026 results (30) USD 400m for Q1 2027, then 350m 15 that the base itself is lifted in 2027 70 15 that it is guided below USD 350m by May 2028 See below
BlueNord synergies, USD 250-300m - About USD 45m a year 50 45 5 Speed of refinancing; Neptune went from 300 to 500 to 600

The dividend is where guidance can exceed our base case, and the reason is in the policy itself (derived). The policy is 25-30% of cash flow from operations after tax "over the cycle". For 2026 the declared USD 1.45bn is about 21-22% of cash flow from operations of about USD 6.6-6.8bn: below the band in the peak year. Reaching 25% would take about USD 0.2-0.25bn more, which is the size of the special dividend Morgan Stanley expected before the company chose USD 50m a quarter instead. For 2028 the USD 1.4bn base is about 35% of cash flow: above the band. A band defined "over the cycle" gives the board cover for both, and the controlling shareholder's position argues for using it in the direction of paying: Eni received EUR 653m from Var in 2025, has nearly tripled its own 2026 buyback to EUR 3.8bn, and plans for cash from its satellites to double by 2030. Against paying more in February: the USD 3.6bn of tax due in H1 2027 and BlueNord's cash consideration and refinancing, all of which liquidity of USD 7.5bn covers. A reduction below USD 350m needs the bear case (30 in 100) and then a board willing to act within a year of the fall; I put the joint probability within 18 months at about 15.

Net reading for the next 18 months. Counting the items that reach earnings: production more likely down than up, unit cost more likely up than down, capex flat to up. Counting the items that reach the share price directly: dividend more likely at or above our base case in the first half of 2027. Var remains the exception in a cohort that guides low and raises (Aker BP raised the low end of its 2026 range; Harbour upgraded three times in 2026; DNO raised).

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out.

  • Long-term production of "about 450 kboepd": reaffirmed 65; softened to a range or "above 400" 25; raised 10, and then only by another acquisition, which is how every previous raise of this target was made (350-400, then above 400, then about 450). A further transaction within 18 months is plausible, about 30 in 100, given the record, the balance sheet and Eni's use of Var as a consolidation vehicle.
  • Capex of about USD 2.5bn a year on average for 2027-2032: reaffirmed with the near years higher 65; raised 30; lowered 5.
  • About eight sanctions and more than 100% organic reserve replacement in 2027: met 65 (the tax deadline pulls sanctions forward; ten in 2025); missed 35.
  • Unit cost for 2028-2029: guided lower if the Balder production unit is retired on time. The sanction was targeted for late 2026 and the compressor work it depends on has moved to "2027 or 2028"; I put retirement in 2028 at about 55 in 100.
  • The dividend base beyond 2028: it holds on the base deck, where free cash flow about equals distributions from FY2029; consensus already has DPS falling to 0.453 in FY2029 (three brokers), a cut of about 10%.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

F2543rd of 54

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the next move in consensus depends on which line is watched, and the lines will part company. On our base case the operating lines still have a little upgrade left for FY2027 (revenue and EBITDA about 6% above consensus, because we keep winter prices through H1 2027), net income is in line, and EPS is 14% too high for FY2027 and 17% too high for FY2028. The EPS gap is not a view about the business: it is the share count, the hybrid coupons and below-EBIT items that brokers' models have not yet caught up with, and it will close by consensus falling. So over the next two to three events I expect sales and EBITDA revisions to be flat to slightly positive if winter gas holds, EPS revisions to turn negative from the first quarter of 2027, and the breadth of revisions, which has been strongly positive, to fade to neutral and then negative. The 81% rise in FY2027 EPS consensus over twelve months is finished.

Where we stand against consensus (the model's comparison, with EPS on my corrected inputs).

Three forces act on consensus EPS, independently of each other.

  1. Mechanical re-basing: down, near-certain, dated. From completion, EPS must be struck on 2,744.8m shares, after about USD 110m of hybrid coupons. Consensus's own FY2028 net income (1,030) gives 0.335 on that basis against the 0.407 FactSet shows, and FY2027's 1,575 gives 0.534 against 0.596: an upper bound of 10% and 18% for the correction. Not every broker needs it: Norne already uses 2,744.8m shares; DNB Carnegie uses 2,496.4m but also leaves BlueNord's earnings out, so its re-basing is roughly neutral; the five lowest FY2027 estimates (0.47-0.53) look closer to the right basis than the seven highest (0.58-0.915). My central estimate of the mechanical effect on the median is therefore about -5% for FY2027 and about -10% for FY2028 (range 0 to -10% and -5 to -18%). Timing: completion around year-end, the FY2026 results on 9 February, and at the latest the first consolidated quarter in late April 2027.
  2. Items below EBIT: down, likely, gradual. Consensus depreciation for FY2027 (3,490) is about USD 0.26bn below ours although BlueNord's assets come in at fair value; and FactSet's FY2027 consensus pre-tax profit (7,122) is above consensus EBIT (6,875), which implies positive net financial items for a company whose finance costs ran at USD 334m a year in Q3 2026 before BlueNord's debt (the two medians come from different sets of brokers, so this is an indication, not a measurement). These are the reason our net income is 4% below consensus on revenue 6% above. They correct as quarters are reported without capitalised interest and with BlueNord consolidated.
  3. The price deck: two-way, and no longer a tailwind. The FY2027 mean rose 22% in the month to 6 November because brokers marked decks towards the curve; eight of the twelve visible estimates are dated 21 October, the day before Brent's peak (USD 111 on 22 October), and a ninth 29 October, the day before Morgan Stanley's "TTF EUR 101". Brent is now USD 91.5 and TTF spot about a fifth lower. If prices hold where they are through the winter, the lower-deck brokers (Clarksons, Norne, Pareto, DNB Carnegie, ING) can still raise FY2027 while the higher-deck ones (Fearnley, Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, Barclays, Nordea, RBC, a restricted broker) trim; if the first step of normalisation arrives early, all cut. FY2028 joined the upgrades only in the last month (+11%); that was the first sign of the deck itself being questioned upwards, and I expect it to stall.

  • Today: FY2027 EPS +22%, +39%, +38% and +81% over one, three, six and twelve months; EBITDA +16%, +27%, +30%, +48%; sales +13%, +23%, +26%, +41%; target price +8%, +16%, +21%.   • Ratings are already moving the other way: the share of buy ratings is 47%, down 5 points in a month and 10 in six; two of the twelve visible brokers rate it Sell, one with the highest estimates (Fearnley) and one with the lowest (DNB Carnegie). The mean target (NOK 56.7) is 14% above the price. Analysts have been raising numbers they do not capitalise.
  • What I expect: the one-month EPS revision turns negative around the turn of the year as the re-basing begins; EBITDA and sales revisions stay near zero while winter prices last; target prices stop rising; the momentum letter moves from B towards C by spring 2027. The events most likely to produce a visible cluster of cuts are the FY2026 results (2027 guidance on volume and cost against consensus volumes of up to 445 kboepd; the H1 2027 cash tax; combined-company models published) and the Q1 2027 results (first consolidated depreciation, finance costs and EPS).
  • What would make me wrong: TTF above EUR 60/MWh through the 2027 refill season and Brent back above USD 100, which is the bull case (20 in 100). Even then, EPS on the right basis is about 0.81 for FY2027, level with FY2026.

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out. FY2028 is covered above. FY2029 consensus (EPS 0.459, net income 1,266, three brokers) has net income rising 23% on revenue up 1%, with EBIT up 18% on depreciation falling to 3,142; the base case has EPS of 0.340 on a flat deck. I put the probability that the FY2029 median is lower a year from now at about 70 in 100, part of it simply through more brokers adding the year. The one item that could lift outer-year estimates is the price deck, if the 2027-2028 LNG additions (about 40 million tonnes a year of capacity in 2027 and 50 in 2028) arrive late.

11d

Current multiple

D2848th of 55Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0735
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

F3545th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the share price is about three-quarters industry-driven: the peer basket explains 74% of its daily variance over the latest six months, with a beta of 1.25, and over twelve months Var has done what a stock with that beta would do. The multiple is less industry-driven than the price. The whole sector is expensive against its own history (the median peer's P/E is at its 78th percentile), and that accounts for about half of the distance between Var's multiple and its own median; the other half is Var's own (99th percentile on trailing earnings, 28% above the median peer's P/E, with its closest peer, Aker BP, at its 43rd percentile). But Var's own half has not been earned by delivery. It is arithmetic: the numerator of the multiple, the share price, follows the sector, while the denominator, Var's forward earnings, is falling faster than peers' for reasons specific to Var. So the multiple is company-specific without being company-driven: the company cannot defend it by executing well, and most of what happens to it will be decided outside the company.

The share price (co-movement table, daily returns, 126-day windows).

  • Latest window (20 May to 12 November 2026): correlation with the equal-weighted basket of 19 peers 0.86, beta 1.25, R-squared 74%, so 26% idiosyncratic. Six-month return +7.8% against +8.9% for the basket; allowing for the beta, Var lagged by about three points (derived).
  • The trend over 24 months: R-squared was 54-64% until February 2026, rose to 71-81% from March to September 2026 (the months after the Hormuz shock, when the sector traded as one position), peaked at 81% in August, and has eased to 74%. Beta ran 1.1-1.35 through 2025, 1.5-1.57 from March to August 2026, and 1.25 now.
  • Closest peers: Aker BP 0.83, BlueNord 0.83 (mechanical, since it trades on the exchange ratio), Equinor 0.82, DNO 0.76, TotalEnergies 0.75, Harbour 0.74, Eni 0.73.
  • Over twelve months Var is up 63.5% against a median of 52.4% for the 19 peers, which is what a beta of 1.25-1.5 would give; there has been no re-rating of Var against its sector.

The multiple.

  • Var: trailing P/E 12.8x, at the 99th percentile of its own history; 24-month-forward P/E on consensus 11.5x, at the 87th percentile of five years, against a five-year median of 6.8x and a two-year median of 9.2x. On our EPS basis, after the corrections above, the two-year-forward multiple is about 14.2x.
  • Peers: median trailing P/E 10.0x; median percentile against own history 78 (Aker BP 43, TotalEnergies 50, Eni 56, Equinor 69, Shell 70, DNO 76, Harbour 78, BlueNord 88).
  • Reading (derived): had Var re-rated only as far as the median peer has, to about the 78th percentile of its own five-year range, its 24-month-forward multiple would be about 9.4x. Of the 4.7 turns between its five-year median and today, about 2.6 are the sector's and about 2.1 are Var's own.
  • A point for the memo's topic on the fair multiple: the guideline fair multiple of 9.41x is almost exactly that figure. The 30% contraction in the multiple inside the model's return therefore removes only Var's own excess and leaves the sector's re-rating in place; the first set's anchors of 6.9-8.1x would remove the sector's as well.

Why Var's own component is passive, not earned. Over twelve months the price rose 63.5% while consensus EPS for FY2027 rose 81% but for FY2028 only 3%. A 24-month-forward multiple looks mostly at FY2028, so the multiple expanded because the market paid for near-term cash (a dividend yield of 9-10% against 3.5-8% at peers) on a price set by the sector, while the earnings two years out stood still. Var's forward earnings are thinner per unit of price than peers' for reasons specific to it: more gas, no hedges, the tax lag, 10% more shares, hybrid coupons. None of these is a strength that execution improves. Aker BP, with the opposite forward shape (cash flow rising into 2027-2028), sits at the 43rd percentile of its own history on a similar P/E.

What this means for the 18 months.

  • Predictability of the share price is the predictability of the sector, amplified. A reading of Brent below USD 85, which the first set identified as the level that removes the reason for the sector position, would act on Var 1.25 times as hard, and on a multiple with further to fall than any peer's.
  • The company-specific quarter has started to work against the stock. The idiosyncratic share rose from 19% in August to 26% now, over the weeks that included DNB Carnegie's downgrade (25 September), the Q3 miss and guidance cut (21 October) and a 12% fall from the 20 October high; the beta has fallen from 1.5 to 1.25 over the same months, so Var is taking less of the sector's good days than it did.
  • The dated company events can move the 26%, not the 74%: the 9 February guidance and dividend framework, BlueNord's completion (free float to about 43%), and any Eni placing, for which the precedent is recent and close (BP sold 5% of Aker BP on 5 November 2026 at a 12% discount, after 2.2% in September at 4%).
  • The technical position is still intact: the price is 10.3% above a 200-day average that has risen in each of the last twelve months, relative strength is at the 88th percentile, and the dashboard's technical letter is A, though the price is below its 50-day average (49.6 against 53.4).

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F2052nd of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Var has already collected the two most reliable sources of E&P outperformance (a step change in volume around first oil, and a corporate-change re-rating) and now fits the pattern that precedes underperformance: peak volume, peak price and peak multiple at once, with consensus EPS falling.

  • Outperformance patterns (sector digest). (1) The commodity tide moves whole cohorts. (2) "A step change in volume is the easiest thing for the market to extrapolate, and the share price pays most in the six to twelve months around first gas": Var's Johan Castberg (March 2025) and Balder X (June 2025) start-ups were followed by a 12-month return of +71% to October 2026. (3) "When planning prices sit far below realised prices, consensus is a floor": in play now. (4) "A yield the market believes is worth far more than one it doubts": the debate on Var. (5) "Synergies stated and then beaten are the tell" for self-funded platforms: Neptune, and the BlueNord test ahead.
  • Underperformance patterns. Buying a cyclical at peak production, peak price and peak multiple ("FALSE FRIEND", the winning hypothesis in the risks report's ACH matrix, with two hard inconsistencies against eight for the bull case); the group's own base rate for a supply shock: realised gas of USD 204/boe in Q3 2022 fell to USD 73/boe by Q1 2026; Equinor's normalisation cycles brought roughly 30% share-price falls within months of commodity peaks; Lundin's production targets were missed by about 10% after its sale to Aker BP (Walker's prior company).
  • Fit. Var's trailing P/E is at the 99th percentile of ten years and its 24-month-forward P/E (11.5x) at the 87th percentile of five; consensus EPS falls 25% in FY2027 and 32% in FY2028; base decline is 8-9% against a 4-6% basin norm. The memo-backed sector returns (Var about -11.9% a year over eighteen months) and the 6 November note ("a 2026 free cash flow yield of about 27 percent is both the market's thesis and the memo's objection") say the same. The counter-pattern that could still apply is the consensus floor: estimates are still rising (FY2027 +39% in three months), and the guide-low habit "survives even when the gap closes".

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F2746th of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: to about NOK 27-35, a fall of 30-45% from NOK 49.6, with a tail into the low NOK 20s if earnings and the multiple trough together; the dividend yield, if the USD 350m quarterly base holds, provides a soft floor in the mid-to-high NOK 30s.

All figures derived from the financials block's ten-year 24-month-forward P/E bands (90th percentile 9.40x "mania", 75th 8.10x "defendable", median 6.90x "prudent", 35th 6.30x "conservative", 10th 5.00x "crash"), today's 11.51x, USD 5.20 per share and NOK 9.54 per USD.

  • Multiple only, earnings unchanged (implied 24-month-forward EPS about USD 0.45): 8.1x gives about NOK 35 (-30%); 6.9x about NOK 30 (-40%); 5.0x about NOK 22 (-57%).
  • Multiple and earnings together: 6.9x on FY2028 consensus EPS of USD 0.407 gives about NOK 27 (-46%); 5.0x on DNB Carnegie's FY2028 EPS of USD 0.35 gives about NOK 17 (-66%), which I treat as the extreme tail.
  • Yield floor: USD 350m a quarter on 2,744.8m shares is about USD 0.51 a share; at a 12-14% yield that is about NOK 35-41 (-18% to -30%). If the base were cut by 30% (DNB Carnegie's earlier expectation), a 12% yield gives about NOK 29.
  • Cross-checks: the 52-week low was NOK 28.44 (dashboard); broker bear cases are NOK 41 (Morgan Stanley, USD 60 oil, 9.5% cost of equity) and NOK 25 (DNB Carnegie's earlier bear case and Jefferies' deep downside at USD 50 oil).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F2451st of 54

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative has moved from "growth and corporate change" to "yield and longevity", and the staircase ahead mostly confirms the second without restoring the first. The near-term steps (Q4 recovery, BlueNord closing, FY2026 results with a dividend framework) are dated and probable but largely priced; the medium-term steps (sanction wave, synergies, Balder cost step-down) are incremental to per-share earnings; and the negative steps (H1 2027 cash tax, LNG wave, end-2027 fiscal test) are equally dated. For the model, the staircase supports holding volumes and the dividend; it does not support raising per-share earnings beyond the price deck.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–82

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly congruent. Every group in both sets tells the same story from a different side (a price-taking, dividend-first producer at peak price, peak volume and peak multiple, whose operations land at the bottom of ranges and whose corporate actions are sound), and the new research written for this set contradicts none of it. The places where the sets jar are few, and all but one resolve on inspection; the one that does not is an arithmetic slip in the second set that changes an interpretation, not a number in the model.

Why I call the congruence high, not merely adequate. A congruent case is one where independent lines of evidence predict each other. Here they do:

  • The management answers (raises what it controls through capital, under-delivers what it controls through operations) predict the guidance record (payouts and long-term targets raised; in-year volume and cost guidance cut), which predicts the second set's leans (volume 4-5% below the company's target, unit cost above guidance, capex at the top, dividend held).
  • The industry answers (price set outside the basin; state takes 78%) predict the economic-strength answers (EPS moves USD 0.117 for each USD 10/boe; cost savings worth 22 cents on the dollar), which predict the second set's finding that cost programmes add only USD 0.02-0.03 a share by FY2029.
  • The risk answers (multiple at the 99th percentile; a fair trough at NOK 27-35) are matched by the model's own return at the guideline multiple (a share price of about NOK 31 at the 18-month column before dividends, NOK 37.6 with them).
  • The first set's group scores all sit between 30 and 50 and the second set scores 37: no group is an outlier that the others would have to explain away.
  • The research written for this set confirms what the sets inferred: no currency hedges of any kind at Q3 2026; the tax payable and the company's own H1 2027 sensitivities; DNB Carnegie's free cash flow of about zero in 2027-2028; Eni's dependence on the dividend (EUR 653m received in 2025; Eni's own buyback raised to EUR 3.8bn); the first call of the 7.862% hybrid on 15 November 2028 (which settles the first set's February 2029 against the second set's November 2028 in the second set's favour).

Where the sets jar, and how each resolves.

  1. When the cash trough falls. The first set put it in H2 2026 to H1 2027; the second set in FY2028. Both are right about the mechanism and differ only on the price path: the trough falls where lagged tax meets lower revenue. On the base deck H1 2027 still carries winter prices, so the USD 3.6bn residual is paid out of high revenue and the shortfall lands in FY2028 (-USD 0.85bn after distributions); on a fast normalisation it lands in FY2027. I side with the second set on the base case, and note that events since 22 October (Brent from USD 111 to USD 91.5 on 11 November; North Sea crude premiums gone) have moved the odds towards the first set's timing (D-04).
  2. The per-share basis. The first set suspected consensus mixed share counts; the second set quantified it; the model then carried a third, wrong basis of its own. Corrected above. After correction the three agree: EPS of 0.51 and 0.34 for FY2027 and FY2028.
  3. The second set's history of depreciation per barrel is wrong, and the interpretation built on it is overstated. It says depreciation is "about USD 24/boe, against about USD 13-14/boe in FY2022-FY2023 (USD 1.56-1.58bn on 202-213 kboepd)". On its own figures that is USD 20-21/boe (1,564-1,580 over 74-78 mmboe; derived). The structural rise is therefore about USD 3-4 a barrel (15-20%), not 75%; depreciation is 2.3 times larger mainly because volume is about twice as large. The consequence: at a like-for-like price the business earns the same operating profit per barrel as before the build-out. FY2023 realised about USD 88/boe (derived, on 213 kboepd) and made an EBITDA margin of 81.2% and an EBIT margin of 58.0%; the FY2027 base case realises USD 87/boe and makes 84.1% and 56.6% (unit cash cost about USD 3 lower, depreciation about USD 4 higher). So the second set's statement that operating leverage is "now higher than at any point in the last six years" for a structural reason does not hold; the leverage is higher in FY2028 because the price is lower, which is what leverage means. Nothing in the forecast inputs depends on the slip (the USD 38/boe of cost ahead of EBIT is built from the forecast, not from history). What it changes for the memo: the build-out did not damage the economics of the barrel; the per-share disappointment sits wholly below EBIT (finance costs once interest stopped being capitalised, hybrid coupons, tax translation, more shares).
  4. The base case is not a bearish forecast, although its narrative reads as one. The first set read consensus FY2027 revenue as about USD 80/boe and called the deck the dominant variable; the second set then chose a deck above consensus for FY2027 (USD 87/boe; revenue 6% and EBIT 12% above consensus). The two are compatible, but the Consumer should be clear about what follows: our negative view of the case does not rest on operating numbers below the street's. It rests on the shape (EPS falls in FY2027 and FY2028 on any deck), on the per-share basis, and on the multiple.
  5. "Consensus is a floor" against "Var does not guide low". The first set scored the sector's guide-low-and-beat habit at 58 and Var's own guidance record at 33-36. Reconciled by the second set: the floor works for Var only through the price deck, never through operations.
  6. The valuation anchors. The first set named 6.9x ("prudent") and 8.1x ("defendable") as the right 24-month-forward multiples for a depleting, no-growth cash stream. The guideline fair multiple in the model is 9.41x, which is the 90th percentile of the same range (9.40x, labelled "mania" on the dashboard). The two sets do not contradict each other, but the memo's topic on the fair multiple should know that the model's -24% is the return at the top decile of the stock's own history; at 8.1x the 18-month share price would be about NOK 26 and at 6.9x about NOK 22 before dividends (derived on the corrected EPS).

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F3842nd of 54

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the narrative is comprehensive on everything above EBITDA and on the dividend, and thin on everything that turns operating profit into earnings per share. The bull case (yield, winter gas, BlueNord, a strong balance sheet) is known to everyone and is in the price; the bear case (peak cash flow, a decline the plateau target ignores, a dividend above cash flow in 2027-2028) is known to everyone and held by a minority. What is not articulated, by any of the four houses whose research I can see, is that consensus EPS sits on the wrong share count, that the stock is therefore more expensive on earnings than the published multiple shows, and that the 2028 dividend is uncovered on every price path, not only the bearish one. The unrecognised dynamics are almost all negative for the shares. On investor commentary I have no direct evidence at all, and say so.

The evidence base, stated plainly. AlphaSense holds research from four covering houses (Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, DNB Carnegie, Norne) and the report covers all four; FactSet shows estimates from twelve brokers, so for eight I can see numbers but not arguments. None of the files contains investor commentary from social media, forums or fund letters. The only window on investors is indirect: Morgan Stanley's feedback note of 17 September 2026 (investors positive on BlueNord and its financing synergies), positioning (energy at the 69th percentile; Var's relative strength at the 88th percentile of the universe; technical letter A), and the ratings drift described in I-02.

Dynamic by dynamic.

Sanction rush, supplier inflation, the end-2027 tax test Generic; Morgan Stanley has capex at USD 2.8bn through 2028 Partly Same

Four things the narrative has not put together, each of which follows from facts that are individually public.

  1. The multiple is higher than it looks. Every published multiple uses consensus EPS. On the company's own EPS definition and the post-merger share count the stock trades on about 14 times earnings two years out, a fifth more than the 11.5 times on screens, at a time when the screens already show the 99th percentile.
  2. The 2028 dividend gap does not depend on the bear case. The debate is framed as bulls who believe prices against bears who do not. But higher prices in 2027 mean higher tax paid in 2028. The cash gap in FY2028 is a feature of the tax calendar on every path; only its funding (cash built in 2026-2027, or debt) differs.
  3. Nothing in the bull case produces growth in EPS. Even on prices that persist through 2027, FY2027 EPS equals FY2026's. The narrative of "consensus is a floor" is about estimates rising towards a level, not about earnings growing from it.
  4. The policy cuts both ways. The 2026 payout is below the 25-30% band and the 2028 payout is above it. The sell-side discusses each end separately (a special dividend that did not come; a cut that may) and has not drawn the conclusion that the board has room to pay more in February 2027 and to hold in 2028.

How well known, in summary. Next 18 months: the price drivers, the dividend debate and the operating record are fully known; the tax lag is known to specialists and in the numbers; the per-share items are known in pieces and assembled by no one. 18 months to three years: the LNG wave is fully known; the end-2027 fiscal test, the November 2028 hybrid call, the 2028 Balder cost step and the Danish licence question (2042 against 2050) are each known to those who look and are not part of any thesis.

What I cannot answer. Whether investors who own the stock hold it for the yield, as a sector hedge or on the BlueNord story, how concentrated the register is outside Eni, what the short interest is, and what retail and social-media commentary says. These matter for how fast the narrative could turn, and I have asked for research on them.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 11 November 2026, 19:41 to 20:02 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Var Energi (VAR-NO): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-11 by the Investment Analyst answering the first set, for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (second set).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

  • Price and units. NOK 49.6 on 2026-11-11, which the financials block equates to USD 5.20 (implied NOK 9.54 per USD). Money is in USD unless stated. Shares: 2,496.4m today; 2,744.8m after the BlueNord combination (248.4m new shares, 9.95% of the enlarged capital), expected to close around year-end 2026.
  • Consensus is the FactSet median at 2026-11-11 on the brokers' adjusted basis: revenue USD 14,368m / 12,866m / 10,772m / 10,900m for FY2026 to FY2029; EBITDA 12,076m / 10,766m / 8,461m / 8,658m; EBIT 8,689m / 6,875m / 5,016m / 5,894m; EPS USD 0.793 / 0.596 / 0.407 / 0.459; DPS 0.540 / 0.530 / 0.505 / 0.453; FCF 3,527m / 1,450m / 954m / 1,371m; capex 2,690m / 2,910m / 2,968m / 2,941m. FY2029 rests on three brokers.
  • Derived figures that are mine, not a source's, are marked "(derived)". They are arithmetic on the files' figures and are given so the Consumer can check and re-use the transmission logic.

The six model-critical judgements, stated once here and argued in the answers below

  1. The price deck is the dominant variable, and consensus is a deck, not spot. FY2026 consensus revenue of USD 14.4bn implies a fourth quarter of about USD 4.4bn (nine months reported USD 9.93bn), which needs both the guided ~410 kboepd and a realised price near USD 115-120/boe, i.e. winter gas near today's TTF (derived). FY2027 revenue of USD 12.9bn is consistent with roughly 430-450 kboepd at about USD 78-82/boe, and FY2028 USD 10.8bn with about USD 69/boe (derived), against USD 102.5/boe realised in Q3 2026 (USD 3,566m on 34.8 mmboe sold). The transmission I recommend the Consumer adopts: each USD 10/boe of realised price moves revenue by about USD 1.6bn a year at ~440 kboepd, EBITDA by about USD 1.5bn, net income by about USD 0.33-0.35bn after the 78% marginal tax, and EPS by about USD 0.12-0.13 on the post-merger share count (derived; consistent with the memo's USD 0.066 per share for USD 5/bbl). The broker range for FY2027 EPS (USD 0.47 to 0.915) is almost entirely a price-deck range, not a view on the company.
  2. Volumes are delivered at the bottom of ranges, from a base that declines 8-9% a year. 2025 landed at 331 kboepd (bottom of the initial range); 2026 guidance was cut from 390-410 to about 390 kboepd and still needs about 410 kboepd in Q4 after 369 in Q3; operated production efficiency fell from 97% (Q1 2026) to 91% (nine months). BlueNord adds about 45 kboepd from closing. I would model the standalone business at about 385-395 kboepd for 2027-2028, not at a full 450 kboepd group plateau, and treat 450 as the upside case.
  3. Unit cost has reset up and is exposed to the krone. Production cost fell from USD 14.1/boe (2023) to 12.8 (2024) and 11.1 (2025), then stalled at 10.9 for nine months of 2026; guidance went from about USD 10 to about USD 11. Roughly 70% of opex and 60% of development capex is in NOK with no currency hedges; BlueNord's unit opex (USD 13 at full Tyra capacity, higher during outages) is dilutive.
  4. Tax is the largest wedge between earnings and cash, and it now also adds EPS noise. The 78% marginal rate makes net income about 22 cents per dollar of operating profit; the switch of functional currency to USD (1 June 2026) left tax balances in NOK, which produced a 65% effective rate in Q2 and 85% in Q3. Cash tax lags income: about USD 2.84bn in H2 2026 (USD 1.14bn paid in Q3, about USD 1.7bn guided for Q4), with the residual on 2026's record income landing in H1 2027.
  5. The dividend is a de facto fixed commitment and the balance sheet is the residual. USD 400m a quarter for Q3 and Q4 2026 (USD 350m base plus USD 50m extraordinary), USD 1.45bn for 2026. On consensus, FCF covers it in 2026 (USD 3.5bn), roughly matches it in 2027 (USD 1.45bn) and falls short in 2028 (USD 0.95bn against at least USD 1.4bn). Between 2019 and 2025, FactSet free cash flow summed to about USD 0.47bn while cash dividends paid summed to about USD 7.3bn (derived): this company has always borrowed to pay its dividend through its build-out. I expect it to do so again rather than cut, unless Brent falls below about USD 60.
  6. Per-share items the model must carry explicitly: 9.95% dilution from closing; hybrid capital of USD 1.75bn classified as equity, whose coupons (about USD 100m a year; the Q2 hybrid interest adjustment was USD 24.5m) are deducted to reach EPS; capitalised interest (USD 219m in 2025 at 6.45%) that flatters reported finance costs; and new senior notes (USD 2.5bn at about 5.3%).

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Closing steer for the second set

  • Build the model around price (three decks), volume (bottom of guidance; 430-440 kboepd group for 2027-2028 with BlueNord from closing), unit cost (about USD 11/boe with a krone sensitivity), depreciation (about USD 23-24/boe), tax (78% marginal with explicit FX noise) and the cash-tax lag (2026 catch-up in H1 2027).
  • Carry the dividend at USD 350m a quarter as a floor with an extraordinary flex in high-price quarters, and let net debt absorb the difference; on consensus this means net debt rising in 2028.
  • Use 2,744.8m shares from closing and deduct about USD 100m a year of hybrid coupons from net income.
  • The single most decision-relevant output is not 2027 EPS but 2027-2028 free cash flow after dividends under each deck, because that is where the valuation and the narrative will be tested.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Var Energi ASA (VAR-NO), a Norwegian Continental Shelf oil and gas exploration and production company, majority-owned by Eni, producing roughly two-thirds liquids and one-third gas from hubs in the Balder area, the North Sea, the Norwegian Sea and the Barents Sea, with a single upstream segment, and combining with BlueNord ASA to add a non-operated 36.8% interest in the Danish Underground Consortium (Tyra, Dan, Gorm, Halfdan) from around year-end 2026

Request: T-P1

Peers: Aker BP (closest Norwegian shelf peer and cost leader, alliance execution model, hedges its NOK spend); Equinor (operator of Johan Castberg and other assets in which Var is a partner, NCS 2035 tie-back cost programme); BlueNord (being acquired; Tyra hub unit costs); DNO (North Sea tie-backs and the Sval Energi integration with a North Sea workforce reduction in early 2026); Harbour Energy (Norway and UK operations, publicised cost reset); OKEA (small Norwegian shelf producer, cost structure of a mature-asset operator) Inputs: What share of Var's unit production cost (guidance raised from about USD 10 to about USD 11 per boe for 2026 at the Q3 2026 results on 21 October 2026) is fixed and what share varies with volume, and what share is NOK-denominated (analysts have estimated roughly 70% of opex and 60% of development capex in NOK)? Why did unit cost fall from USD 14.1 per boe (2023) to USD 12.8 (2024) and USD 11.1 (2025) and then stall at USD 10.9 for the first nine months of 2026, and how much of the 2026 increase was the krone, unplanned maintenance, and fuel and power prices (management attributed about USD 0.5 per boe to currency)? What were the "year-end savings" that delivered USD 10 per boe in Q4 2025, and were they repeatable? What is the timing and Var's net share of the roughly USD 130m a year gross opex reduction from retiring the Balder FPU in 2028 after the Jotun FPSO debottlenecking? What explains the fall in operated production efficiency from 97% in Q1 2026 to 91% for the first nine months, and what did peers' efficiency do over the same period? How has depreciation per boe moved since 2023 as Balder X and Johan Castberg entered the base, and how will purchase-price accounting on BlueNord affect group depreciation? Under the Norwegian 78% marginal tax system, the 86.9% deduction for pre-2023 projects (expiring for projects submitted after end-2027) and immediate expensing, how much of a dollar of opex or capex saving reaches post-tax cash flow, with precedents? What are the components and phasing of the USD 250-300m post-tax BlueNord synergies over 2027-2032, and how did the Neptune Energy Norge synergies rise from USD 300m guided to USD 600m delivered, and over what time? What is BlueNord's unit opex at full Tyra capacity (reported as about USD 13 per boe) versus during outages, and how does it change the combined group's unit cost? What did Norwegian shelf margins and unit costs do in the 2015-2016 and 2020 down-cycles and in the 2022-2024 normalisation, for Var's predecessor companies and for Aker BP and Equinor? How are harsh-environment rig day rates (reported at USD 450,000-525,000) and subsea capacity constraints flowing into Var's well and development costs, including the Deepsea Bergen fixture for 2028-2031? Which cost lines have peers actually cut (Aker BP's alliances, Equinor's 50% tie-back cost and lead-time target, Harbour's reset, DNO's workforce reduction), what did each deliver against what was promised, and which lines proved sticky (salaries, tariffs, carbon tax)? Focus: How much of Var's roughly USD 11 per boe cost base and its USD 2.5-2.7bn annual development capex is genuinely reducible, how quickly, and what each dollar is worth after Norwegian tax; and how strongly operating profit moves with realised price and volume given largely fixed NOK costs and unit-of-production depreciation.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Aker BP (realised a higher overall price than Var in Q1 2026 and prices liquids against physical dated Brent); Equinor (largest Norwegian gas seller, day-ahead exposure, markets the state's volumes); BlueNord (Danish gas sold through Nybro and Den Helder, heavily hedged); DNO (Norwegian gas sold under a contract with ENGIE, oil with ExxonMobil and Shell); Harbour Energy (Norway and UK gas exposure); Eni (Var's majority shareholder and main off-taker, buying most of Var's sales) Operating conditions: a supply-shock market with Brent above USD 100 per barrel and European gas (TTF) near EUR 80-90 per MWh, as in the second half of 2026 with the Strait of Hormuz disrupted and EU gas storage about 74% full before winter; a rapidly rising gas market in which month-ahead indexation lags spot; a rapidly falling market after a geopolitical de-escalation or a Hormuz reopening; normalisation to the long-term decks used by brokers (DNB Carnegie USD 75 Brent and USD 12 per mmbtu gas from 2028; Norne USD 75 Brent for 2027-2028); a Brent below USD 60 downturn; a strong krone; the 2027-2028 wave of new LNG supply from the United States and Qatar; and the EU phase-out of Russian LNG (from January 2027) and pipeline gas (by late 2027) Inputs: How have Var's realised crude prices tracked dated Brent (a USD 6 per barrel premium in Q2 and Q3 2026; management expected USD 6-10 in Q4 2026; Norne cited cargoes up to USD 20 above Brent), and how did the differential behave in 2022-2025? How have realised gas prices tracked TTF and NBP (Q3 2026 about USD 108 per boe, about USD 18 below the spot reference; Q2 2026 about USD 1 above; Q1 2026 about USD 7 below; Q3 2022 USD 204 per boe), and what share of gas is sold fixed-price or year-ahead (about 30% of Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 at about USD 82 per boe), month-ahead, and day-ahead? What do Var's long-term gas contracts (about 65% of gas sold under long-term offtake with market-linked pricing and price-review provisions, per earlier disclosures) contain on indexation, nomination flexibility, review clauses and lags, including the 12-year contract with Eni for up to 5 bcm? How concentrated are sales on Eni entities (about USD 2.85bn of USD 3.57bn of Q3 2026 petroleum revenue) and what are the pricing terms and credit terms of those related-party sales? Is the claim that gas marketing earns about USD 1.3bn above spot over two-year cycles (Norne Securities) substantiated, and how did it behave in falling markets? Why did Aker BP realise about 6% more than Var in Q1 2026 (crude quality, gas mix, transport optionality or commercial practice)? How does Var's hedging (collars on about 13% of remaining 2026 post-tax oil near USD 75 per barrel and about 15% of Q4 2026 post-tax gas near USD 83 per boe) and its absence of currency hedges compare with peers, and how do hedges roll? How will BlueNord's gas sales, hedges and transport (three Ørsted pipelines) change the group's realisation profile? What do customers, competitors and the sell-side currently say about Norwegian pipeline-gas pricing power against LNG later in the decade? Focus: How closely, and with what lag, Var's realised oil and gas prices follow the benchmarks in rising, falling and normalising markets, given its fixed-price, indexation and related-party sales structure; and whether its crude and gas marketing premiums are structural or market-dependent.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Var's 2027-2028 cash flow depends on several self-reinforcing and self-correcting dynamics (the Norwegian cash-tax lag, a fast base decline, a dividend historically funded by debt, and krone strength moving with oil prices) that the earnings lines do not show. Peers: Aker BP (same fiscal regime, peak capex now, hedged NOK spend); Equinor (largest taxpayer on the shelf, publicly described tax-lag effects on 2027 cash flow); BlueNord (high-payout harvesting of a mature hub); DNO (tax-lag effects on reported free cash flow after the Sval acquisition); Harbour Energy (delayed cash tax payments flattering first-half 2026 free cash flow) Inputs: The Norwegian petroleum tax instalment schedule and how a high-price income year (2026) is settled in the following year, with Var's own figures (cash taxes of about USD 0.85bn in Q2 2026 and USD 1.14bn in Q3 2026, about USD 1.7bn guided for Q4 2026) and peers' precedents after 2022; the base decline of Var's mature portfolio (analysts have cited 8-9% a year, against a commonly cited 4-6% for mature North Sea hubs) and the reinvestment, well count (more than 50 new wells planned in 2026) and sanctions (about eight targeted for 2027) needed to hold a plateau; the history of distributions exceeding free cash flow at Var since 2019, funded by borrowing, and whether the 25-30% of post-tax operating cash flow "through the cycle" policy has ever constrained the dividend; the interaction of a stronger krone with high oil prices (unhedged NOK costs; tax balances held in NOK after the change to a USD functional currency on 1 June 2026, which produced an 85% effective tax rate in Q3 2026); whether hub consolidation around Gjøa and Balder makes successive tie-backs cheaper and faster (selected subsea lead times reported cut from 30 to 18 months); whether the rush to sanction before the end-2027 expiry of the temporary tax regime is tightening supplier capacity and raising costs for all operators; and whether Var's high equity valuation, used as currency for BlueNord, could reverse if the yield case is questioned. Focus: Which of these loops will dominate Var's 2027-2028 free cash flow after distributions, and what evidence would show them strengthening, stalling or reversing.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Var's earnings per share depend heavily on items below operating profit (tax translation, hybrid coupons, share count, depreciation, finance costs) on which published broker forecasts may use different bases. Peers: Aker BP (same tax regime, 76% quarterly effective tax rate and impairment noise in 2026); Equinor (Norwegian tax lag and currency effects); BlueNord (its tax losses, debt and Danish tax regime that Var will absorb); DNO (106.3% effective tax rate in 2025 on Norwegian tax balances); Harbour Energy (Norwegian and UK tax interaction) Inputs: What drives Var's effective tax rate after the functional-currency change on 1 June 2026 (65% in Q2 2026, 85% in Q3 2026, against a 78% marginal rate), and how does it respond to USD/NOK moves (Nordea forecasts USD/NOK at 9.82 in mid-2027 and 9.19 by end-2027)? How do brokers treat the coupons on USD 1.75bn of hybrid capital classified as equity (EUR 750m at 7.862% to February 2029; EUR 750m at 4.95% to July 2031; a Q2 2026 hybrid interest adjustment of USD 24.5m) in EPS, and what happens at the first call date? Which share count does each broker use for 2027 (2,496.4m today; 2,744.8m after the 248.4m BlueNord shares), and from what closing date? What depreciation per boe should be expected for the combined group, including purchase-price accounting on BlueNord? What production profile do brokers assume for 2027-2028 standalone and for the DUC (Norne Securities raised its forecasts to 445 kboepd for 2027 and 450 kboepd for 2028 after the BlueNord announcement)? Focus: A reconciliation from consensus operating profit to earnings per share for 2027-2028, covering the effective tax rate and its currency sensitivity, finance costs, hybrid coupons and the share count, and where the brokers' assumptions differ.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 02:11 to 02:31 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Var Energi (VAR-NO): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 2026-11-12 by the Investment Analyst answering the second set, for the Investment Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

  • Units and bases. Money in USD millions unless stated; per-share figures in USD. Price NOK 49.6 (2026-11-11), USD 5.20 on the financials block's own pair. Revenue, EBITDA and margins in my base case are on FactSet's fundamentals basis (FY2025 revenue USD 7,966m, EBITDA USD 6,467m, margin 81.2%), because that is the history the Financial Analyst's model holds; the brokers' basis is about 1.5% higher on revenue and about 1.4 points lower on EBITDA margin (FY2025: USD 8,096m and 78.8%). Company EBITDAX (before exploration expense) is close to FactSet operating EBITDA (Q3 2026: USD 3,073m against 3,095m), and FactSet's D&A line carries expensed exploration, so FactSet EBIT matches company EBIT.
  • Share count. 2,496.4m to the end of 2026; 2,744.8m from BlueNord completion, which I assume on or about 1 January 2027 (company: "around year-end 2026").
  • EPS definition used throughout. Net income less hybrid coupons (about USD 110m a year from 2027), divided by the share count in issue. This is the company's reported basic EPS definition. It is not the definition most brokers appear to use (see P-08).
  • Free cash flow definition used throughout. Operating cash flow after cash tax and cash interest, less all capital spending including capitalised and expensed exploration and abandonment spend, less lease payments; before hybrid coupons, dividends and acquisitions. This is stricter than FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex), by about USD 0.4-0.6bn a year.
  • Everything marked "(derived)" or presented as my base case is my own arithmetic on the files' figures. The base-case price deck, volumes and the full set of lines are stated once in the table below and re-used, unchanged, in every answer.

Two notes on comparing with consensus. First, my operating lines are at or slightly above consensus in FY2027-FY2028 because I hold H1 2027 prices higher than the brokers' decks (winter 2026-27 with storage about 74% full before winter and the Russian LNG ban from January 2027), while my volumes are below theirs (428 against Norne's 445 kboepd). Second, my EPS is 14% below consensus in FY2027 and 17% below in FY2028 although my net income is within 4% of consensus net income: the gap is the per-share basis, not the business (P-08).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Var reports one upstream segment (Norway); from BlueNord completion (assumed 1 January 2027) Denmark is a second producing area. Revenue by product and area, my base (USDm, derived):

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Norway liquids (crude and NGL, about 67% of standalone volume) ~9,350 7,840 6,820 6,840 6,820
Norway gas (about 33%) ~4,730 4,590 3,170 2,910 2,680
Denmark (BlueNord's 36.8% of the DUC; 58% liquids, 42% gas; after BlueNord's mandatory hedges) - 1,205 1,070 1,020 990
Group 14,080 13,630 11,065 10,765 10,460

Margins: Norway EBITDA margin about 86% (FY2026), 85% (FY2027), 82% (FY2028) at a cash cost of about USD 11/boe plus overhead; Denmark EBITDA margin about 73% (FY2027) and 71% (FY2028) at an all-in cash cost of about USD 19/boe during the Tyra ramp (BlueNord: USD 13/boe direct field opex at full capacity, USD 23/boe all-in in Q3 2026). If Var books BlueNord's out-of-the-money hedges as a liability at completion, Denmark's reported FY2027 revenue and EBITDA would be up to about USD 0.2bn higher, with no change to cash.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Price deck. Brent about USD 100/bbl in Q4 2026; FY2027 USD 81 (Q1 90, Q2 82, Q3 77, Q4 75); USD 72 in FY2028-FY2029; USD 73 in FY2030. TTF about EUR 85/MWh in Q4 2026; FY2027 EUR 51.5 (Q1 70, Q2 50, Q3 44, Q4 42); EUR 35, 32 and 30 in FY2028-FY2030. Crude premium to dated Brent USD +7/bbl in Q4 2026, +2-3 in FY2027, +1 from FY2028. Gas: about 30% of Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 standalone gas fixed at about USD 82/boe; the month-ahead lag subtracts about USD 18/boe in Q4 2026 and adds about USD 4-5/boe in H1 2027 as prices fall. Realised price about USD 100, 87.4, 71.3, 69.4 and 68.7 per boe. Each USD 10/boe is about USD 1.55bn of revenue and EBITDA, USD 0.32bn of net income and USD 0.116-0.118 of EPS.
  • Volumes. Standalone 386 kboepd in FY2026 (Q4 395, against about 410 needed for the company's about 390 guidance), 385, 382, 383 and 377 in FY2027-FY2030; BlueNord 43, 43, 42, 40 kboepd from FY2027. Group 428 (FY2027), 425, 425, 417 kboepd, against the company's long-term target of about 450.
  • Costs. Standalone unit production cost USD 11.2/boe (FY2027), 10.9 (FY2028, Balder FPU retirement worth USD 117m a year net from about mid-2028), 10.6 (FY2029), 10.9 (FY2030, carbon tax and wages); BlueNord about USD 19/boe all-in; other operating cost and overhead about USD 300m a year. Krone: 70% of opex and 60% of development capex in NOK, unhedged.
  • D&A includes expensed exploration and dry wells (FactSet's EBIT matches company EBIT on this basis): about USD 24/boe in FY2027 including BlueNord at about USD 19/boe after fair-value accounting, drifting to about USD 23/boe. Consensus D&A (USD 3,490m and 3,318m in FY2027-FY2028) looks USD 0.26-0.33bn light.
  • Net financial items include non-cash accretion on about USD 5.3bn of abandonment obligations (about USD 190-200m a year), interest on about USD 7.9bn of senior notes after the USD 2.5bn issue at about 5.3%, BlueNord's debt (USD 725m reserve-based loan, USD 400m BNOR18 at 7.875%, USD 300m BNOR17 hybrid at 12%) until refinanced, and interest income on cash. FY2026 includes about USD 0.2bn of net FX gains already booked. They exclude the coupons on Var's USD 1.75bn of equity-classified hybrids (about USD 97m in FY2026, 110m in FY2027-FY2028, about 90m from FY2029 if the 7.862% tranche is refinanced at its late-2028 first call), which must be deducted from net income before EPS; if the model does not deduct them, its EPS will be about USD 0.04 a share above mine in every year from FY2027.
  • Tax rate. 78% marginal; FY2026 75.4% reflects nine months at 74% (FX gains on NOK tax balances in Q2); 79.0% in FY2027 for expected krone strength (Nordea USD/NOK 9.19 by end-2027) and partial deductibility of finance costs; BlueNord's Danish 64% regime pulls the group rate down slightly from FY2028.
  • Cash tax lag (not an input line, but it drives free cash flow). Cash tax paid USD 4,430m, 6,165m, 4,870m, 3,945m and 3,790m against tax charges of 6,411m, 5,714m, 3,794m, 3,635m and 3,433m. If the model sets cash tax equal to the tax charge, its free cash flow will be about USD 2.0bn below mine in FY2026, about USD 0.45bn above in FY2027, about USD 1.07bn above in FY2028 and about USD 0.3bn above in FY2029-FY2030. H1 2027 carries about USD 3.5bn (range 3.2-3.8bn) of residual tax on 2026 income.
  • Dividends. Declared USD 300m, 350m, 400m, 400m for the four quarters of 2026; USD 400m for Q1 2027 then USD 350m a quarter. DPS is entered on the paid-in-year basis FactSet and consensus use (FY2026 USD 1,350m over 2,496.4m shares; FY2027 USD 1,500m over 2,744.8m; then USD 1,400m).
  • Share count. +248.4m BlueNord consideration shares (issued for BlueNord shares, not for cash) at completion, entered in FY2027 on the assumption that completion falls on or about 1 January 2027; the FY2027 EPS should use 2,744.8m for the whole year. If completion falls in late December 2026, the shares belong in FY2026's closing count with no material effect on FY2026 weighted EPS.
  • Capital spending is broader than FactSet's history line: development capex (about USD 2.65bn in FY2026-FY2028, 2.5bn in FY2029, 2.4bn in FY2030, including BlueNord's USD 100-150m), all exploration spend (capitalised and expensed: about USD 0.27bn in FY2026, 0.20-0.25bn thereafter) and abandonment spend (about USD 0.2bn plus BlueNord and the Balder FPU removal, 0.25-0.30bn). On FactSet's narrower definition FY2027 capex would be about USD 2.75-2.9bn, close to consensus USD 2.91bn.
  • Working capital on FactSet's definition (receivables plus inventories less payables): USD 880m (7.3%) at Q3 2026, falling as Q3's receivables were collected; 5.0% at end-2026 on high December prices, then 4.0-4.5%. Receivables factoring (USD 348.6m sold at 30 September 2026) is assumed constant.
  • Lease payments cover rigs, helicopters and supply vessels (lease liabilities USD 169.2m at September 2026); higher from FY2028 with the Deepsea Bergen fixture (about USD 475,000 a day, 2028-2031), most of whose cost is shared with partners and capitalised into wells.
  • Acquisitions less disposals. FY2026: USD -70m (Pandion and the DNO swap, as booked). FY2027: USD -1,204m = BlueNord cash consideration USD -204m (NOK 1,964m), plus BlueNord net debt assumed of about USD -1,300m (reserve-based loan, BNOR18 and the BNOR17 hybrid, net of BlueNord cash), entered here so that the model's net debt is right although it is not a cash payment, less about USD +300m of Goliat (20%) and Fenja (25%) disposal proceeds (up to USD 350m announced).
  • Buybacks less new shares issued for cash. FY2026 USD +889m = the EUR 750m 4.95% hybrid issued in April 2026 (about USD 882m, classified as equity, no ordinary shares issued) plus about USD 7m of employee share issuance. It is entered so that the model's FY2026 net debt matches the reported reduction; no buybacks are expected.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue. My inputs grow from FactSet fundamentals revenue (FY2025 USD 7,966m; nine months of 2026 USD 9,928m, equal to the company's petroleum revenue). Consensus is on the brokers' basis, about 1.6% higher (FY2025 USD 8,096m). My FY2026 revenue of USD 14,080m on the brokers' basis would be about USD 14,300m against consensus USD 14,368m.
  • EBITDA. Company EBITDAX (before exploration expense) is close to FactSet operating EBITDA (Q3 2026 USD 3,073m against 3,095m), and my margins are on FactSet's basis. The brokers' basis runs about 1.4 points lower (FY2025 78.8% against 81.2%), so my FY2027 84.1% is about 82.7% on the consensus basis (consensus 83.7%) and my FY2028 80.9% about 79.5% (consensus 78.5%).
  • EBIT and D&A. FactSet's D&A carries expensed exploration and dry wells, so FactSet EBIT matches company EBIT; my D&A follows FactSet.
  • Net income. Company net income is after FX gains and losses in finance items and after FX effects on NOK tax balances in the tax line (Q2 2026 +USD 160.3m, Q3 -USD 71.8m). Brokers' adjusted figures appear to remove finance FX but not, in most cases, the tax translation. My forecasts carry no FX after Q3 2026.
  • EPS. The company's basic EPS deducts hybrid coupons (Q2 2026 adjustment USD 24.5m) and divides by shares in issue; mine follows it. Consensus EPS appears to mix bases: FY2028 consensus EPS (USD 0.407) is almost exactly consensus net income (USD 1,030m) over the pre-merger 2,496.4m shares with no hybrid deduction (0.413); on 2,744.8m shares after hybrid coupons the same net income gives 0.335. DNB Carnegie uses 2,496.4m shares and standalone volumes; Norne uses 2,744.8m. FactSet's own adjusted diluted EPS (FY2025 USD 0.152) and the Viewforth definition (0.145) strip FX and are not comparable with the brokers' 0.310.
  • Free cash flow. FactSet and consensus define free cash flow as operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex. Mine also deducts capitalised exploration and other intangibles, expensed exploration (which sits below EBITDA), abandonment spend and lease payments, so it is about USD 0.4-0.6bn a year lower than the same cash flows on FactSet's definition. On FactSet's definition my FY2027 free cash flow would be about USD 2.3-2.5bn against consensus USD 1.45bn, and FY2028 about USD 1.1-1.3bn against 0.95bn.
  • Net debt. The company's NIBD (USD 3,110m at Q3 2026) excludes the USD 1.75bn of hybrids and, apparently, leases; FactSet's net debt (USD 4,958m at Q3 2026) includes the hybrids and leases.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

EPS is net income less hybrid coupons over 2,496.4m shares (FY2026) and 2,744.8m shares (FY2027 onwards). Free cash flow is operating cash flow after cash tax (with the instalment lag) and cash interest, less all capital spending including exploration and abandonment, less lease payments; before hybrid coupons, dividends and acquisitions.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Var Energi ASA (VAR-NO), a Norwegian Continental Shelf oil and gas exploration and production company majority-owned by Eni, reporting a single upstream segment (roughly two-thirds liquids and one-third gas from the Balder area, North Sea, Norwegian Sea and Barents Sea hubs), combining with BlueNord ASA to add a non-operated 36.8% interest in the Danish Underground Consortium (Tyra, Dan, Gorm, Halfdan; operated by TotalEnergies) from around year-end 2026

Request: T-D1

Peers: Aker BP (closest Norwegian shelf peer, cost leader, alliance execution model, hedges NOK spend); Equinor (operator of Johan Castberg and other Var partner assets, NCS 2035 tie-back cost programme); BlueNord (being acquired; Tyra hub reliability and unit cost under a non-operated structure); DNO (integration of Sval Energi and North Sea tie-backs); Harbour Energy (integration of Wintershall Dea and a publicised cost reset); OKEA (mature-asset Norwegian operator); Lundin Energy (precedent: production delivery after its 2022 sale to Aker BP, under Var's current chief executive's former leadership); Neptune Energy Norge (precedent: Var's own 2024 integration and synergy delivery) Operating conditions: supply-shock prices with Brent above USD 100 and TTF near EUR 80-100 per MWh (H2 2026, Strait of Hormuz disrupted, EU storage about 74% full before winter); normalisation to brokers' long-term decks (USD 70-75 Brent, about USD 10-12 per mmbtu gas from 2028); a Brent below USD 60 downturn; a strong Norwegian krone with unhedged NOK costs; the end-2027 expiry of the temporary Norwegian tax regime and the tax reform commission reporting in late 2027; a tight supplier market (harsh-environment rigs at USD 450,000-525,000 a day, subsea contractors booked into 2027-2028) Inputs: Can Var hold its long-term production target of about 450 kboepd (company, with BlueNord) against a base decline analysts cite at 8-9% a year, given that 2025 production landed at the bottom of its initial range and 2026 guidance was cut from 390-410 to about 390 kboepd (21 October 2026)? Is a return of unit production cost to about USD 10 per boe plausible after 2026 guidance was raised to about USD 11, and how much depends on the Balder FPU retirement in 2028 (about USD 130m a year gross opex) versus uptime? What explains operated production efficiency falling from 97% (Q1 2026) to 91% (first nine months), and how quickly have peers recovered from similar slips? Is the capex cap of about USD 2.5bn a year on average for 2027-2032 credible given Var's history of operated-project overruns (Balder X, Balder Future) and the pre-2028 sanction rush? Can BlueNord's post-tax synergies of USD 250-300m over 2027-2032 be delivered or beaten, as Neptune's were (USD 300m guided, USD 600m delivered), and how did comparable non-operated acquisitions perform? Is a dividend of USD 350-400m a quarter sustainable against the stated policy of 25-30% of post-tax operating cash flow through the cycle, given that distributions exceeded free cash flow in most years since 2019? Is the target of about eight project sanctions and more than 100% organic reserve replacement in 2027 consistent with scheduled reserves and risked resources for 2026-2029 falling from 527 to 438 mmboe between December 2025 and September 2026? Focus: For each of Var's expected changes (plateau volumes, unit cost recovery, BlueNord synergies, dividends against the stated policy), whether the record shows Var has already demonstrated the capability it needs (in transactions, synergy capture, project start-ups, operational uptime, in-year volume and cost delivery), and how often producers attempting the same change from a similar starting point delivered it.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Aker BP (same fiscal regime, peak capex now, hedged NOK spend, lagged cash tax of USD 1.0bn and 1.5bn guided for Q1 and Q2 2027); Equinor (largest shelf taxpayer, publicly described H1 2027 tax payments above H2 2026; share price fell about 30% within seven months of the 2022 peak); BlueNord (Danish 64% hydrocarbon tax, tax-loss carry-forwards, mandatory hedging); DNO (106.3% effective tax rate in 2025 on Norwegian tax balances; hedges tax instalments in NOK); Harbour Energy (UK and Norwegian tax interaction, front-loaded UK tax schedule); OKEA (small-cap sensitivity to outages and impairments) Operating conditions: a gradual normalisation through 2027 as new US and Qatari LNG arrives; a sudden normalisation in early 2027 after a Hormuz reopening or a mild winter; prices persisting near late-2026 spot through 2027; krone strength (Nordea forecasts USD/NOK 9.82 in mid-2027 and 9.19 by end-2027) while oil falls; repeated unplanned outages at concentrated hubs (Balder area and Johan Castberg carry about 40% of Var's output) Inputs: How much of the difference between Var's 2027-2028 and its 2021-2025 earnings and free cash flow comes from each of: realised price; the Norwegian tax-instalment lag (cash taxes of about USD 0.85bn in Q2 2026 and USD 1.14bn in Q3, about USD 1.7bn guided for Q4, with the balance of 2026 tax due in H1 2027); tax translation on NOK tax balances after the 1 June 2026 change to a USD functional currency (65% effective tax rate in Q2 2026, 85% in Q3); depreciation per boe after Balder X, Johan Castberg and BlueNord purchase-price accounting; net finance costs after interest capitalisation ended (USD 219m capitalised in 2025) and the USD 2.5bn senior-note issue; hybrid coupons on USD 1.75bn of equity-classified hybrids; the 248.4m BlueNord consideration shares; and volumes? How wide was the dispersion of EPS and free cash flow outcomes for Norwegian and UK producers in the 12-24 months after the 2022 price peak, which events widened or narrowed it, and how often did forecasts resting mainly on the price deck and tax timing prove right? How do effective tax rates of Norwegian producers with USD functional currency and NOK tax balances respond to a 5-10% krone move, quarter by quarter? Focus: Whether Var's next 18 months rest on one or two drivers (the price deck and the timing of the 2026 tax settlement) or on many, and how wide and how skewed the plausible range of 2027 EPS and 2027-2028 free cash flow after distributions is, given those drivers.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Aker BP (guides low and lands at or above the top of its range; Q3 2026 cost guidance raised); Equinor (Q4 2026 shelf tax of NOK 93bn expected after NOK 61bn in Q3); Harbour Energy (upgraded 2026 guidance twice); DNO (raised North Sea guidance in 2026); BlueNord (base assets met or beat guidance for 17 consecutive quarters; Q3 2026 production 4% below guided midpoint); OKEA (mature-asset operator with volatile uptime) Operating conditions: the winter 2026-27 gas market (EU storage low, Russian LNG banned from 1 January 2027); a physical North Sea crude market loosening (on 9 November 2026 Morgan Stanley reported dated Brent below front-month futures with 36 cargoes unsold, and Equinor cut its Oseberg premium from USD 8.40 to USD 1.10 per barrel); normalisation from Q1 2027 (Deutsche Bank); a strong krone; BlueNord completion around year-end 2026 Inputs: What will Var guide at the FY2026 results on 9 February 2027 for 2027 production (combined with BlueNord), unit cost, development capex, exploration, abandonment, cash tax by quarter and the 2027 dividend framework, and how does that compare with the company's record (2025 production at the bottom of the initial range; 2026 cut to about 390 kboepd; 2026 unit cost raised from about USD 10 to 11; dividends raised four times in 2026)? Will Q4 2026 production reach the roughly 410 kboepd needed for the 2026 guidance, given Q3 at 369 kboepd? Is the USD 50m extraordinary quarterly dividend likely to continue into 2027, and will any special dividend accompany the FY2026 results (Morgan Stanley expected one earlier)? How are the sell-side FY2027 and FY2028 forecasts constructed: which brokers use 2,496.4m shares and which 2,744.8m, which deduct the hybrid coupons from EPS, which include BlueNord's volumes, debt and purchase-price depreciation, and how would FactSet consensus EPS move once all contributors re-base after completion? Why does FactSet's FY2027 consensus pre-tax profit (USD 7,122m) exceed consensus EBIT (USD 6,875m)? FactSet FY2027 EPS consensus rose 22% over one month, 39% over three and 81% over twelve to 11 November 2026, and FY2028 rose 11% over one month: how much of this is price deck, how much FX and tax, and how much a change of share basis? Where does consensus sit against the company's Q3 2026 run-rate and against the forward curve? Focus: Whether Var's guidance at the next earnings events is more likely to be met, cut or raised on volume, cost and cash tax, and whether the next move in consensus EPS is set more by the price deck or by brokers re-basing their models for BlueNord's shares, debt, depreciation and the hybrid coupons.
